I think the organiser should ask what each task requires, when support is available, and which examples justify a description. The meeting showed that one person can combine a preference with an obligation. It did not prove that Rosa always works alone or that Daniel refuses help.”

**Clause check:** **that would appear on its website** identifies the poster; **whose subject he did not know well** links the presentation to its subject. **who wanted…** adds information about named Rosa. The final two sentences bound the interpretation.

**Worked correction:** Before: “Rosa, that wanted a clear message, explained.” After: “Rosa, who wanted a clear message, explained.” A non-defining clause about a person uses **who**, not **that**.
</details>

## Safety and progress note

Use fictional names and settings whenever a prompt feels personal. Keep each original and revised recording locally according to your own privacy choices. These three sessions are a bounded speaking practice slice; they do not complete a month, certify CEFR, or approve a course package.


<a id="sec-ielts-phase-3-reading-cycle3-reading-md"></a>
## Source 024 — `IELTS/Phase-3/reading/cycle3-reading.md`

# Reading — Cycle 3: traits, states, and careful interpretation

This cycle covers D9–D11 in three separate original English passages. Each passage is fictional and uses personality language as a prompt for evidence-based reading; no character is diagnosed and no label is treated as a measurement. Spend 50 minutes per day: preview and glossary (5), first reading (12), four questions (15), evidence check and hints (10), transfer writing (8). The optional glossary supports access and is not a new core vocabulary list. Aim for 3/4 independently, then use the progressive hints and explain the evidence again.

Grammar link: [whose, where, when](#sec-ielts-phase-3-grammar-03-relative-adverbs-whose-md) (when, where, whose, and preposition meaning). The cycle uses **whose** before a noun, **where** for a place relationship, and **when** for a time relationship. The mapping of Cycle 3 to the wider 24-day guide is owned by the separate guide; this file does not assign test day 12.

**Cách tự học:** mỗi ngày đọc một bài D9/D10/D11. Xem từ hỗ trợ5phút, đọc lần đầu12phút, trả lời bốn câu15phút, đối chiếu bằng chứng và dùng gợi ý10phút, viết chuyển giao8phút. Mỗi câu đúng ý và có bằng chứng nhận1điểm; câu gồm nhiều ý chỉ nhận điểm khi đủ ý. Mục tiêu3/4 ở lần chưa xem gợi ý/khóa. Giữ điểm lần đầu; sau gợi ý sửa bằng lời riêng và ghi vì sao sai. Bài viết80–120từ cần hai quan sát, cấu trúc đang học và giới hạn của suy luận; chưa đạt thì sửa trước khi chuyển bài. Bài đọc hư cấu không đo tính cách hoặc chứng nhận B2.

## D9 — The rehearsal room that changed its question

**Optional glossary:** rehearsal = buổi tập; cue = tín hiệu; reserved = dè dặt; revise = sửa lại; evidence = bằng chứng.

### Passage (fictional, 582 words)

The old town theatre was preparing a short performance about a lost suitcase. Mira joined the group two weeks before opening night. The director described her as reserved because she usually sat at the edge of the room and wrote notes while others improvised. At the first rehearsal, Mira declined the invitation to invent a character on the spot. She said she needed to understand the scene before choosing a voice. The director wrote “quiet” beside her name on the cast list, although he did not ask what kind of work helped her think.

The next afternoon, the cast rehearsed a scene in which three travellers argued beside a railway noticeboard. Mira was responsible for the noticeboard, whose handwritten departures had to make sense whenever the actors pointed at it. She noticed that the times in the draft contradicted one another. One train was said to leave at six, while a later line implied that it had already gone at five. Mira did not interrupt the actors. She marked the conflict and waited until the break, when the director asked for practical suggestions. She then proposed a sequence of times and explained why each clue needed to appear.

The proposal solved one problem but created another. The actors had been using the inconsistent times to make the argument funny, and they worried that a tidy board would remove the joke. Mira listened, then suggested that the contradiction could remain if a character deliberately read the wrong line. The director asked her to demonstrate the idea. She stepped into the scene, read the noticeboard in a dramatic voice, and paused at the exact moment the audience would notice the mistake. The cast laughed, not because Mira had suddenly become a different person, but because the structure gave her a clear place to act.

During the following rehearsal, a visiting student asked Mira to lead a warm-up game. The game required instant instructions and fast changes. Mira hesitated, lost the first round, and handed the activity back to the student. A cast member later said this proved that she lacked confidence. The director disagreed with the certainty of that conclusion. He had seen her speak clearly about a prepared problem, perform a planned cue, and struggle with a sudden game. Those observations could describe different states under different demands; they could not by themselves settle a permanent trait.

On opening night, a prop suitcase broke when its handle caught on a chair. Mira noticed the problem before the audience did. She moved the suitcase behind a bench, whispered a new cue to the actor, and pointed to the revised route. The adjustment was small, yet it prevented a pause from becoming a visible mistake. Afterwards, the director thanked her. Mira replied that the written notes had helped, but the cast had also made room for questions. She did not claim that preparation solved every difficulty. She said that knowing when she could observe, when she could test an idea, and when she needed another person’s cue made participation easier.

The director replaced “quiet” on the cast list with a fuller note: “watch what happens when the task, time, and invitation change.” The replacement was not a new label. It was a reminder to collect more than one kind of evidence before describing a person. Mira’s work had included private checking, public explanation, collaborative revision, and a quick practical response. The theatre still needed a performance, not a personality verdict.

### Questions

1. Why did Mira wait until the break before raising the timetable problem? Give two details.
2. What changed when Mira demonstrated the revised joke, and what does the passage say this does **not** prove about her?
3. Which two observations led the director to reject the claim that the warm-up proved a permanent lack of confidence?
4. In “the noticeboard, whose handwritten departures had to make sense”, what does **whose** refer to, and why would **where** be wrong there?

<details><summary>Hidden key and evidence</summary>

1. She did not want to interrupt the actors, and the director invited practical suggestions at the break; she had marked the contradiction first so she could explain it precisely.
2. A clear structure let her demonstrate the timing and the cast could see the joke; it does not prove she became a different person or that preparation explains every situation.
3. She spoke clearly about a prepared problem and performed a planned cue, while the warm-up demanded instant changes. The contrast shows task-dependent states, not a permanent verdict.
4. **Whose** refers to the noticeboard and introduces the noun “handwritten departures,” showing possession/association. **Where** needs a place relationship and cannot replace a possessive determiner before a noun.

</details>

**Scaffold and scoring:** First underline two observable details for each answer (0–2 evidence points), then explain the contrast between a trait label and a temporary state (0–2), and finally check the relative word and commas (0–2). Use the progressive hints only after a first attempt: (1) underline “did not interrupt” and “during the break”; (2) compare the two rehearsal conditions; (3) list evidence before and during the game; (4) look at the noun immediately after the blank.

**Transfer (80–120 words):** Describe a fictional learner whose behaviour changes across two study tasks. Include one **whose**, one **when**, and two concrete observations; end by stating what the observations cannot prove. Revise once for comma and relative-clause accuracy.

## D10 — The survey with three unfinished columns

**Optional glossary:** survey = bảng hỏi; hesitant = do dự; anonymous = ẩn danh; pattern = khuôn mẫu; interpret = diễn giải.

### Passage (fictional, 570 words)

At Northbridge College, a student committee wanted to improve the quiet study room. Its first plan was based on a quick survey containing three adjectives: focused, sociable, and independent. Students could circle one word and add a comment if they wished. The committee expected the results to reveal which type of learner needed which facility. When the first responses arrived, the secretary, Jo, noticed that many students had circled more than one word in their comments. One person chose “independent” but asked for a weekly planning partner. Another chose “sociable” but said that noise made concentration difficult.

Jo asked the committee to read the comments before drawing a conclusion. The chair argued that the labels made the results easy to display. A bar chart could show how many students selected each word, whereas a page of comments looked untidy. Jo agreed that the chart was convenient, yet she pointed out that it hid the questions the students were answering. Someone might call herself independent because she liked choosing her own method, not because she wanted to work alone. Someone else might call himself sociable because he enjoyed exchanging explanations, not because he could study beside constant conversation.

The committee tested this possibility in a small fictional follow-up meeting. Hana, whose survey answer said “independent,” described a project in which she had designed the first draft alone. She had then asked two classmates to challenge her assumptions before submitting it. Daniel, who had selected both “sociable” and “focused,” explained that he preferred a short discussion followed by forty minutes of silence. Neither student rejected the adjective, but each attached a different meaning to it. Their examples also changed with the task: planning, checking, and memorising did not demand the same arrangement.

The chair suggested adding a question about frequency: “How often do you work alone?” Jo said this was useful but incomplete. A person might work alone because the room was crowded, because a deadline was near, or because no partner was available. Frequency described an event; it did not automatically explain a preference. The group therefore added questions about the condition, the choice, and the result. Students could report when they concentrated best, where interruptions occurred, and whose feedback they trusted for a particular assignment.

The revised form took longer to complete. Some students complained that the extra questions were less attractive than a three-word quiz. Jo replied that a quick answer was not always a simple answer. If the college bought partitions based only on the first chart, it might solve noise for one activity and make collaborative planning harder. The committee created two trial zones instead: a silent area with movable screens and a discussion table that could be reserved for a limited period. It also invited students to record what they tried and what changed.

After a month, the committee found no single “learner type.” Several students moved between zones during the week. One student who had described herself as quiet became the most active questioner during peer review. Another who had chosen sociable preferred a silent corner while revising numbers. The committee did not treat these changes as contradictions. It treated them as information about demands, choices, and available support. The final report kept the bar chart, but placed it beside selected comments and a warning: a label can organise a conversation, yet it cannot replace the evidence that gives the label meaning.

### Questions

1. Why did Jo think the bar chart hid an important distinction? Give two different meanings of “independent” or “sociable” from the passage.
2. What did the revised form ask students to record, and how did those questions improve the committee’s decision?
3. Why did the later examples not count as contradictions of the original labels?
4. In “Students could report when they concentrated best, where interruptions occurred, and whose feedback they trusted,” distinguish the three embedded interrogative clauses from relative clauses. What question does each clause answer?

<details><summary>Hidden key and evidence</summary>

1. The chart counted words but not their meanings. “Independent” could mean choosing one’s method while still seeking challenge; “sociable” could mean exchanging explanations while needing quiet for concentration.
2. They recorded conditions, choices, results, times, places, and trusted feedback. This linked a report to a situation and helped the committee trial both silent and discussion spaces.
3. The students behaved differently under different tasks or conditions; the passage presents labels as context-sensitive descriptions, not fixed predictions.
4. These are embedded interrogatives, not relative clauses: **when they concentrated best** answers “at what time?”, **where interruptions occurred** answers “in what place?”, and **whose feedback they trusted** answers “the feedback of which person?”. They do not modify an antecedent noun in this sentence. A relative example from the passage is “Hana, **whose survey answer** said ‘independent,’ …”, where **whose + noun** modifies Hana.
</details>

**Scaffold and scoring:** Mark the antecedent (if there is one) before labelling a clause (0–2), give one condition-and-result pair from the passage (0–2), and check the distinction between an embedded question and a relative clause (0–2). Progressive hints: (1) find the two comments that qualify a label; (2) locate “condition, choice, and result”; (3) compare planning, checking, and memorising; (4) ask “what time, what place, whose person?” before calling a clause relative.

**Transfer (80–120 words):** Write a short recommendation for a study space using two observations, one limitation of a label, and one sentence with **where** or **when**. Add a sentence with **whose + noun** and revise for clear reference.

## D11 — The interview transcript with a missing margin

**Optional glossary:** transcript = bản chép lời; margin = lề; tentative = tạm thời; inference = suy luận; revise = chỉnh sửa.

### Passage (fictional, 561 words)

The editorial club was producing a guide for new volunteers. Priya interviewed members about how they handled difficult feedback. She planned to summarise each conversation in the margin of her transcript, but the first interview made her question that method. Leo arrived late, glanced at the recorder, and answered in short phrases. Priya wrote “defensive” beside his name. When she asked whether he preferred praise or criticism, he said, “Neither, if the comment is vague.” He then asked to see the sentence she wanted him to discuss.

The sentence came from a draft introduction. It claimed that every reliable volunteer responded calmly to correction. Leo had crossed out “every” and written “a volunteer may need time.” Priya thought his change sounded argumentative. Leo explained that the club’s previous editor had once corrected three paragraphs in a single message. He had read the message just before a deadline and answered too quickly. The editor later clarified which changes were essential and which were optional. Leo accepted the essential changes after that clarification and kept the optional ones for a later revision.

Priya asked whether this meant he disliked teamwork. Leo laughed. He said that he liked a shared task when each person knew whose decision was final at each stage. During a poster project, the designer, whose visual plan was strongest, decided the layout, while the writer decided the wording. They consulted one another when a change affected both. Leo said he became impatient only when several people edited the same sentence without saying who would choose the final version. The description sounded more precise than “defensive,” so Priya crossed out her first margin note.

The second part of the interview concerned a missed deadline. Leo had promised to check references by Friday, but he sent his comments on Monday. Priya asked what had happened. He showed her a calendar in which a family appointment and an unexpected shift were marked. He had also underestimated the time needed to verify a quotation. He admitted that the delay affected the team and suggested a remedy: in future, he would report a risk on Wednesday rather than wait until Friday. Priya recorded the delay, the causes he gave, and the proposed change separately. She did not turn the episode into proof that he was either unreliable or perfectly organised.

When the transcript was reviewed, another editor questioned Priya’s revisions. “You removed a useful personality description,” the editor said. Priya answered that a short label might be useful as a search term, but only if the notes beside it preserved the evidence. She kept a temporary tag, “asks for specific feedback,” and linked it to the sentence about “every,” the example of divided editing responsibility, and the late reference check. The tag remained open to revision because one interview could not show how Leo would respond in every setting.

The guide eventually used a dialogue box rather than a list of types. It showed an interviewer asking, “What would make this feedback actionable?” and a volunteer answering with a request for examples, priority, and timing. Priya added a note when the question was asked and where the answer changed the draft. The box did not promise that all volunteers needed the same support. It showed how a careful question could turn an impression into information that a team could use.

### Questions

1. Which three items does Priya explicitly link to the temporary tag “asks for specific feedback”? What limitation does she state about that tag?
2. Why did Leo’s late reference check not prove that he was simply unreliable? Give two contextual details and the action he proposed.
3. What did the dialogue box add that a list of personality types would not add?
4. In “the designer, whose visual plan was strongest, decided the layout,” explain the meaning of **whose**. Then express the same information in two independent sentences using “the visual plan of the designer.”

<details><summary>Hidden key and evidence</summary>

1. Priya explicitly links the tag to the sentence containing “every,” the example of divided editing responsibility, and the late reference check. She keeps it temporary because one interview cannot show how Leo would respond in every setting.
2. A family appointment and unexpected shift occupied the calendar, and he underestimated quotation-checking time; he proposed reporting a risk by Wednesday. These explain the episode without excusing its effect or proving a fixed trait.
3. It preserves the question, context, answer, and practical implication, showing how an impression can become usable information rather than predicting behaviour from a type.
4. **Whose** links the designer to the noun phrase “visual plan” and expresses association/possession. Two clear independent sentences are: “The visual plan of the designer was strongest. The designer decided the layout.” The version with a non-defining relative clause is more concise; **where** would be wrong because the relationship is not a place.
</details>

**Scaffold and scoring:** Circle the three facts explicitly linked to the tag (0–2), separate cause from effect and proposed remedy (0–2), and check the antecedent and commas in the **whose** clause (0–2). Progressive hints: (1) find the three linked items named in the review paragraph; (2) separate cause, effect, and remedy; (3) compare a label with the dialogue’s question-and-answer structure; (4) identify the noun after whose and preserve the non-defining commas.

**Transfer (80–120 words):** Write a fictional interview exchange about feedback. Include one inference supported by two observable details, one **whose** clause, and one sentence with **when** or **where**. State one limitation of your interpretation and revise the punctuation.

## Cycle-wide language check

Underline one **whose + noun**, one **where**, and one **when** in each answer set. Explain the preposition meaning in at least two alternatives: *the room where we met / the room in which we met* and *the period when we revised / the period during which we revised*. Keep defining and non-defining commas accurate. A correct answer may use a semantically equivalent sentence when it identifies the same evidence.

## Source and boundary note

The passages, questions, keys, hints, and transfers are original course writing. They contain fictional observations only and make no empirical claim about personality, MBTI, Big Five validity, diagnosis, or learner level. Grammar guidance is inherited from the accepted grammar evidence and its British Council source; this reading slice does not create a new grammar verification claim. This file does not certify CEFR, test readiness, course completion, or learner attainment.


<a id="sec-ielts-phase-3-writing-cycle3-writing-md"></a>
## Source 025 — `IELTS/Phase-3/writing/cycle3-writing.md`

# Writing — Cycle 3: place, time, reason, and association

This cycle practises **where**, **when**, **why**, and **whose** through fictional descriptions of teams and study settings. Every task requires a 200–250 word response. Spend 50 minutes: interpret the brief and plan (8), draft (15), check antecedents and punctuation (10), revise (10), and self-score (7). The models are original course writing and are examples of reasoning, not mandatory wording.

## Cách học và tự chấm

Dành 50 phút mỗi ngày: đọc yêu cầu và lập dàn ý (8), viết bản nháp (15), kiểm tra danh từ được bổ nghĩa và dấu câu (10), sửa bài (10), tự chấm (7). Viết 200–250 từ trước khi mở bài mẫu. Chỉ gọi **where/when/why** là mệnh đề quan hệ khi nó bổ nghĩa cho danh từ chỉ nơi chốn, thời gian hoặc lý do; một mệnh đề **when** chỉ hoàn cảnh của hành động có thể là mệnh đề trạng ngữ. Với mỗi tiêu chí: 2 điểm nếu đáp ứng đầy đủ và chính xác, 1 nếu chỉ đáp ứng một phần hoặc còn lỗi nhỏ, 0 nếu thiếu hoặc sai nghĩa. Tổng 12 điểm dùng để chọn phần cần luyện lại, không chứng nhận trình độ. Giữ bản nháp và bản sửa, rồi giải thích ít nhất một thay đổi.

## D9 — An observation memo about two study settings

### Task

Write a 200–250 word observation memo for a fictional study coordinator comparing how one learner works in two settings. Include one **where** clause for a place, one **when** clause for a time, and one **whose + noun** clause expressing association. Explain two observable differences, give one practical suggestion, and state why the observations do not prove a fixed personality description. Use clear defining or non-defining commas.

### Process and self-check

1. Plan the two settings, the actions observed, and the suggestion that follows from them.
2. Draft one sentence with **where**, one with **when**, and one with **whose + noun**; underline the noun each clause relates to.
3. Revise: replace **where** with *in which* once to test the place meaning, and replace **when** with *during which* to test the time meaning. Check commas and remove any diagnosis or forecast.

**Visible progressive hints:** (1) choose a room for **where** and a session/day for **when**; (2) put a noun after **whose**, such as “whose notes”; (3) end with a sentence limited to this observation and these settings.

**Transfer and self-score:** Write one alternative sentence using *in which* or *during which*. Score 0–2 each for task/length, evidence, **where/when** meaning, **whose** form and punctuation, practical suggestion, and limitation/revision (12 points). Record one antecedent you checked.

<details><summary>Original model, annotations, and full criteria</summary>

### Original model (227 words)

Observation memo: Minh’s study settings

Minh prepared a reading map in the library where the group kept its quiet tables. He spoke confidently when the task had a clear sequence and he had time to mark the questions first. On the afternoon when the library hosted a children’s event, he moved to a smaller room and wrote his suggestions before joining the discussion. In the room in which he worked, fewer interruptions made it easier for him to compare two possible routes through the text.

Minh’s notebook, whose pages were divided into evidence and questions, helped the group notice what he was responding to. During a later meeting, the room became lively and another learner asked for an immediate decision. Minh did not give the same kind of explanation, but he pointed to the map and asked the group to choose one route. The change is an observation about a task and a setting. It does not prove that Minh is permanently quiet, dependent, or confident.

I suggest giving him the question list before a busy meeting and allowing a short written preparation period. The coordinator should also record what happens in a different room, when the task is less structured, and when another person needs an immediate answer. These notes may help the group plan fairly, but one week of observations cannot explain every future response.

### Annotation and worked revision

- **where the group kept its quiet tables** modifies **library** and expresses place; **when the library hosted…** modifies **afternoon** and is a time relative clause. By contrast, **when the task had a clear sequence…** modifies the circumstances of **spoke** and is an adverbial time clause; there is no time-noun antecedent in that sentence. “The afternoon during which…” is a formal alternative to the relative clause.
- **whose pages** expresses the notebook–pages relationship. It is not *who’s* and must be followed by a noun.
- A weak sentence said, “Minh is introverted.” The revision names the room, task sequence, and preparation time, then limits the conclusion.

### Rubric and acceptable alternatives (12 points)

- 0–2 task and length: memo, 200–250 words, comparison, suggestion, and limit.
- 0–2 evidence: two observable differences tied to settings or times.
- 0–2 **where/when**: each modifies the right place or time noun.
- 0–2 **whose** and punctuation: noun follows **whose** and commas match meaning.
- 0–2 practical suggestion: follows reasonably from the evidence.
- 0–2 revision and boundary: replaces a fixed label with a bounded observation.

Accept *the room in which…* for **where** and *the period during which…* for **when**. Accept any fictional learner and settings if the antecedents are clear. Do not accept **where** after a non-place noun or **whose** without a following noun.
</details>

## D10 — A project note about a shared folder

### Task

Write a 200–250 word project note for a fictional team that is revising a shared folder. Explain why one label in the folder should be replaced by a description of actions and conditions. Use two **whose** clauses, one defining and one non-defining, plus one **why** clause that gives a reason. Include a workable process change and a limitation about what the note can establish.

### Process and self-check

1. Plan the old label, two pieces of evidence, the reason the team needs a new description, and the process change.
2. Draft a defining **whose** clause that selects a person or document, then a non-defining **whose** clause with commas for a named item.
3. Revise the **why** clause: it should answer “for what reason?”, not replace a place or time clause. Check that each **whose** is followed by a noun.

**Visible progressive hints:** (1) use “the editor whose checklist…” to identify one editor; (2) use commas around extra information about a named folder; (3) connect the reason to a process, such as recording conditions before interpreting a label.

**Transfer and self-score:** Rewrite the old label in one sentence without any personality adjective. Score 0–2 each for task/length, evidence, defining/non-defining **whose**, reason meaning, process clarity, and limitation/revision (12 points). Note whether each **whose** clause is D or ND.

<details><summary>Original model, annotations, and full criteria</summary>

### Original model (231 words)

Project note: revising the “reliable” folder

The team should replace the label “reliable” with notes about the conditions in which each task was completed. We began with the editor whose checklist included the source date and the person responsible for the next check. She usually finished a review before the meeting, but she requested more time when the shared folder contained unfamiliar files. This evidence suggests a useful process, not a permanent type.

The folder, whose first page lists the current questions, should contain a short record of each revision. Writers can note when a change was requested, where the supporting evidence appears, and why the change was accepted or postponed. The reason why we need this record is that a quick label can hide the difference between a missed deadline, an unclear instruction, and a task that requires outside information.

For the next two weeks, the coordinator should ask the person who changes a file to add one reason and one next action. Another editor should read the note before the meeting, and the team should invite corrections from the writer. If the record becomes more complete, we may say that the process produced fuller notes during that period. We cannot say that the label was a measured personality quality or that the process alone caused every improvement. The folder should help the team ask a fairer question next time.

### Annotation and worked revision

- **the editor whose checklist…** is defining: it selects the editor. **The folder, whose first page…** is non-defining because the folder is already named; commas add information.
- **why we need this record** modifies **reason** and is a reason relative clause. The following **that a quick label can hide…** supplies the content of the reason; it is not another **why** relative. In “note … why the change was accepted”, **why** introduces an embedded question rather than modifying a reason noun.
- A weak sentence said, “The checklist made the editor reliable.” The revision reports fuller notes during a period and names other possible factors.

### Rubric and acceptable alternatives (12 points)

- 0–2 task and length: project note, 200–250 words, old label and process change.
- 0–2 evidence: two conditions or actions, not unsupported adjectives.
- 0–2 defining/non-defining **whose**: selection and extra information are distinguished.
- 0–2 **why**: gives a reason and has a clear antecedent or clause relationship.
- 0–2 process: feasible recording, checking, and correction steps.
- 0–2 limitation and revision: avoids claiming measurement or single-cause effect.

Accept a **why** clause after *the reason* or a natural sentence beginning *This is why…* when the reason is clear. Accept a person or document as the **whose** antecedent. The non-defining version needs commas; the selecting version normally does not.
</details>

## D11 — A formal reflection on a workshop room

### Task

Write a 200–250 word formal reflection after a fictional workshop moved rooms. Explain how place and time affected the group’s work and recommend one change for the next session. Use one **where** clause, one **when** clause, one formal **preposition + whom/which** pattern, and one **whose** clause. Include two evidence points and a final sentence that limits interpretation. Avoid a comma splice and avoid treating a reduced or relative clause as proof of a permanent trait.

### Process and self-check

1. Plan the original room, the new room, the time-linked change, and the recommendation.
2. Draft a formal sentence such as “the colleague to whom we sent the draft” or “the table at which we worked”; identify its preposition relationship.
3. Revise every comma: use a conjunction to coordinate complete ideas, or separate them into sentences. Check that **where** is for place, **when** for time, and **whose** before a noun.

**Visible progressive hints:** (1) choose a real place noun for **where** and a meeting period for **when**; (2) ask “to whom/at which what?” before selecting the formal pattern; (3) split any sentence that joins complete clauses without a conjunction.

**Transfer and self-score:** Convert the formal preposition pattern into a natural final-preposition version and explain the meaning is unchanged. Score 0–2 each for task/length, evidence, **where/when**, formal preposition and **whose**, recommendation, and limitation/revision (12 points). Keep the original and revised sentence.

<details><summary>Original model, annotations, and full criteria</summary>

### Original model (226 words)

Formal reflection on the feedback workshop

The workshop began in the room where the group usually practised short presentations. The tables were wide, and the wall displayed a schedule that everyone could read. On the afternoon when construction started nearby, however, several participants could not hear the recording. We moved to a smaller room in which the shelves reduced the noise, although the narrow tables made it harder to spread out drafts.

The colleague to whom we sent the revised plan suggested a useful compromise. We kept the quiet room for listening and used the library table at which the group could compare sentences during the final discussion. A participant whose notes separated observations from interpretations noticed that the change of room had affected the evidence, not necessarily the group’s interest. Another participant, who had spoken quickly in the first room, wrote a question before speaking in the second.

For future sessions, I recommend reserving the quiet room when a recording is central and scheduling the discussion at a time when construction is unlikely. We should ask participants what they could hear and record which task became difficult. The reflection describes this workshop only. It does not prove that a room determines how anyone participates, or that one schedule will solve every problem. A clear plan should leave space for another observation and a later revision.

### Annotation and worked revision

- **where the group usually practised** modifies **room**; **when construction started** modifies **afternoon**. The formal **in which** and **at which** clauses express place relations.
- **to whom we sent the revised plan** is formal; the natural alternative is “the colleague who we sent the revised plan to”. **Whose notes** places a noun after **whose**.
- A comma plus **and** is valid coordination in “The tables were wide, and the wall displayed…”. A weak draft said, “The room was noisy, we moved.” The revised “The room was noisy, so we moved” supplies the conjunction; two separate sentences would also work. This fixes a real comma splice without treating correct comma-plus-conjunction coordination as an error.

### Rubric and acceptable alternatives (12 points)

- 0–2 task and length: formal reflection, 200–250 words, evidence and recommendation.
- 0–2 evidence: two place/time-linked observations.
- 0–2 **where/when**: accurate place and time relations.
- 0–2 form: formal preposition + **whom/which**, plus **whose + noun**.
- 0–2 recommendation: feasible and connected to the evidence.
- 0–2 limitation and revision: avoids causal/personality overclaim and repairs coordination.

Accept a natural final preposition when meaning is preserved. Accept a different room or schedule if the place/time relation is explicit. Do not mark a sentence wrong merely because it coordinates complete clauses with comma plus **and**.
</details>

## Boundary note

The three tasks, model responses, annotations, and rubrics are original course writing. People, teams, rooms, and observations are fictional. This file makes no diagnostic, empirical personality, CEFR, band-score, learner-attainment, or course-readiness claim. It is an author-side writing resource awaiting independent review.


<a id="sec-ielts-phase-3-speaking-cycle3-speaking-md"></a>
## Source 026 — `IELTS/Phase-3/speaking/cycle3-speaking.md`

# Speaking — Cycle 3: whose, where, when, and why

Đây là ba buổi tự học D9–D11, tiếp nối Cycle 2 bằng **whose/where/when/why**. Dùng lại core vocabulary hiện có; không thêm bộ từ mới. Nhân vật và hành vi là hư cấu, hoặc bạn có thể chọn bối cảnh hư cấu hoàn toàn. Không cần chia sẻ đời tư, không có xác minh audio bên ngoài, và không có tuyên bố CEFR hay sẵn sàng cả tháng.

Grammar reference: [relative adverbs and whose](#sec-ielts-phase-3-grammar-03-relative-adverbs-whose-md). Optional vocabulary support: [Cycle 3 vocabulary](#sec-ielts-phase-3-vocabulary-03-personality-cycle3-md).

## Quy trình 50 phút cho mỗi buổi

1. Phút 0–8: đọc prompt, chọn 2–3 chi tiết và lập ba gạch đầu dòng.
2. Phút 8–18: chuẩn bị đúng 1 phút, nói 60–90 giây, rồi nghe lại bản ghi và ghi nhận chỗ khó hiểu trong thời gian còn lại.
3. Phút 18–28: chọn hai lỗi có thể sửa, ghi câu trước và câu sau; đối chiếu gợi ý khi cần.
4. Phút 28–40: chuẩn bị lại đúng 1 phút, nói lại 60–90 giây, nghe lại và ghi một thay đổi hiệu quả.
5. Phút 40–50: tự chấm 10 điểm, điền error log và chọn bước tiếp theo.

**Rubric 10 điểm:** prompt và 2–3 chi tiết (0–3: 0 thiếu, 1 ít chi tiết, 2 đủ hai chi tiết, 3 cụ thể và có quan hệ điều kiện); clause/antecedent (0–3: 0 thiếu/sai, 1 mơ hồ, 2 hầu hết đúng, 3 đúng và giải thích được vai); core vocabulary (0–2: 0 không dùng được từ core đúng nghĩa, 1 có từ đúng nhưng còn lỗi hoặc thiếu ngữ cảnh, 2 dùng đúng nghĩa phù hợp); dễ hiểu và tự sửa (0–2: 0 chưa hoàn tất, 1 hoàn tất nhưng sửa mờ, 2 bản thu lại rõ hơn). **7–10:** chuyển tiếp; **5–6:** sửa hai lỗi và thu lại; **0–4:** dùng dàn ý ba câu và luyện lại. Accent không bị trừ điểm. Chỉ ghi lỗi làm nghĩa khó hiểu.

**Error log:** `Ngày/buổi:` · `Prompt:` · `Lỗi 1/câu trước:` · `Sửa 1:` · `Lỗi 2/câu trước:` · `Sửa 2:` · `Thay đổi trong bản thu lại:` · `Điểm/10 và bước tiếp theo:`

## D9 — A place and a changing routine

**Prompt:** Describe a fictional learner whose routine changes in two places or at two times. Include 2–3 details, one **where**, one **when**, and one question you would ask next. Speak 120–180 words.

<details><summary>Gợi ý 1</summary>

put **where** after a real place noun and **when** after a time noun
</details>
<details><summary>Gợi ý 2</summary>

replace one clause with *in which* or *during which* to check meaning
</details>
<details><summary>Gợi ý 3</summary>

explain why the setting matters without predicting a fixed trait.
</details>

<details><summary>Full model and worked correction</summary>

**Natural model (131 words):**

“I want to describe Linh, a fictional learner who studies in two different places. The library where she usually reads has a quiet corner, and she writes detailed notes there. On the afternoon when the discussion group meets, she moves to a larger table and asks a partner to compare examples with her. The change does not show that Linh is always reserved or always sociable. It shows that the task and the room give her different opportunities. I would ask which part of the routine helps her check an idea before speaking. The group could record another session in which the room is busy and then compare the notes. My account is limited to these observations, so I would describe a pattern of choices rather than a permanent personality type.”

**Clause check:** **where she usually reads** modifies **library**; **when the discussion group meets** modifies **afternoon**. **in which the room is busy** modifies **session**. Both **in which** and **during which** can describe that event; this is not a rule requiring **during which** for every session. The model’s **when** clause has the explicit time antecedent **afternoon**, whereas a stand-alone “When the group meets, …” can introduce an adverbial time clause.

**Worked correction:** Before: “the afternoon where the group meets.” After: “the afternoon when the group meets.” The antecedent is time, so **when** is accurate.
</details>

## D10 — A notebook whose questions changed

**Prompt:** Describe a fictional team member whose notebook changed a discussion. Include 2–3 details, two **whose** clauses (one defining and one non-defining), one **reason why** relative clause, and one embedded question. Speak 120–180 words.

<details><summary>Gợi ý 1</summary>

put a noun directly after **whose**
</details>
<details><summary>Gợi ý 2</summary>

use a defining **whose** clause to identify one person and commas for extra information about a named notebook
</details>
<details><summary>Gợi ý 3</summary>

place relative **why** after **reason**; contrast it with “She asked why …”, an embedded question.
</details>

<details><summary>Full model and worked correction</summary>

**Natural model (138 words):**

“The member whose questions were most specific asked the group to describe evidence before choosing a label. Her notebook, whose first page listed three follow-up questions, stayed open beside the draft. She asked why one comment called a paragraph confusing and invited the writer to identify the exact sentence. The reason why the group changed its discussion was that the questions turned a broad reaction into a task that someone could revise. I would ask whether the same method helps when the team has little time. One meeting cannot prove that the member always asks precise questions or that her notebook causes better decisions. It can show that, in this discussion, the notebook gave the group a useful place to record reasons. The next meeting should keep the questions and record one example that complicates the first impression.”

**Clause check:** **whose questions were most specific** identifies the member; **whose first page…** adds information about named notebook and needs commas. **why the group changed its discussion** modifies **reason** and is a relative clause. **why one comment called…** is an embedded question after **asked**, with no noun antecedent to modify. Both **whose** clauses are followed by a possessed noun.

**Worked correction:** Before: “The notebook, whose was useful, changed the talk.” After: “The notebook, whose first page listed questions, changed the talk.” **Whose** needs a noun after it.
</details>

## D11 — A place, a time, and a hidden question

**Prompt:** Describe a fictional interview in a place and at a time that affected the conversation. Include 2–3 details, one **where**, one **when**, one **reason why** relative clause, and one embedded question. After recording, explain separately why the embedded question is not a relative clause. Speak 120–180 words.

<details><summary>Gợi ý 1</summary>

name the place and time before choosing **where/when**
</details>
<details><summary>Gợi ý 2</summary>

put relative **why** after **reason**, and contrast it with an embedded “asked why …” question
</details>
<details><summary>Gợi ý 3</summary>

mark the embedded question by asking “what information did the interviewer want?” rather than looking for an antecedent noun.
</details>

<details><summary>Full model and worked correction</summary>

**Natural model (146 words):**

“The interview took place in a library where the group could speak without the noise of the main hall. It happened on the evening when the editor had time to read the draft before asking questions. She asked why the writer had removed a sentence, and then asked what evidence the sentence was meant to show. The writer pointed to the original note and compared it with the draft. The reason why the editor suggested a revision was that the sentence sounded certain although the example was limited. The editor suggested a revision and asked when the writer could check the new wording. I would keep a note of the reason, the proposed change, and the time of the next check. This fictional interview shows how a careful question can improve a draft, but it does not prove that one conversation explains the writer’s future decisions.”

**Clause check:** **where the group could speak…** modifies **library**; **when the editor had time…** modifies **evening**. **why the editor suggested a revision** modifies **reason** and is a relative clause. **why the writer had removed…** and **what evidence the sentence was meant to show** are embedded questions after **asked**, with no noun antecedent to modify. **when the writer could check the new wording** is another embedded question; it differs from the time-relative clause attached to **evening**.

**Worked correction:** Before: “the evening where the editor read.” After: “the evening when the editor read.” A time noun takes **when**. Do not replace the embedded question with a relative label.
</details>

## Safety and progress note

Use fictional settings whenever the prompt feels personal, keep recordings private, and choose locally stored files only if that suits you. These sessions practise speaking form and revision; they do not certify CEFR, test readiness, audio quality, course completion, or a full 24-day curriculum.


<a id="sec-ielts-phase-3-reading-cycle4-reading-md"></a>
## Source 027 — `IELTS/Phase-3/reading/cycle4-reading.md`

# Reading — Cycle 4: omitted and reduced relative clauses



This cycle covers D13–D15 with three original fictional passages about teamwork, feedback, and personality descriptions. The texts use defining relatives, omitted object pronouns, and reduced clauses in context. They do not diagnose anyone or claim that a label predicts behaviour. Spend about 50 minutes per day: preview and glossary (5), first reading (12), four questions (15), evidence check and hints (10), and transfer writing (8). Try the questions before opening each hidden key. Grammar reference: [Grammar 4 — Omission and reduced relative clauses](#sec-ielts-phase-3-grammar-04-omission-reduced-clauses-md).





## Cách học và tự kiểm tra



Mỗi ngày dành khoảng50phút: xem tiêu đề và đoán nội dung5phút, đọc lần đầu12phút, trả lời bốn câu15phút, đối chiếu chi tiết và dùng gợi ý10phút, viết đoạn chuyển giao8phút. Giữ đáp án đóng trong lượt đầu. Với mỗi câu, ghi một chi tiết từ bài đọc rồi giải thích bằng lời của bạn; không cần chép nguyên văn. Khi gặp relative clause, xác định danh từ được bổ nghĩa và chủ ngữ của động từ trước khi quyết định bỏ đại từ. Tự chấm mỗi câu0–2:2đúng cả ý và bằng chứng/giải thích,1đúng một phần,0sai hoặc không có câu trả lời; tổng8điểm là công cụ tự sửa, không chứng nhận trình độ. Sửa câu sai bằng một ví dụ mới rồi làm đoạn chuyển giao80–120từ.



## D13 — The project board with two kinds of silence



### Passage (fictional, 578 words)



At the start of the community theatre project, the team described Nari as quiet. She rarely interrupted a meeting, and she preferred to mark suggestions on the shared board before speaking. Arun, the coordinator, worried that the group would overlook ideas that Nari had noticed. He asked her to lead the first planning review, but she requested a different arrangement: everyone would read the notes that she had collected, then each person would choose one question to discuss. The request changed the meeting without making Nari perform a louder personality.



The notes concerned a scene that the director wanted to shorten. Nari had listed the entrances that the audience might miss, the jokes that the actors could deliver without a pause, and the prop change that the stage crew had rehearsed twice. She had not written that the director was wrong. Instead, she separated observations from choices. The scene that the director wanted to cut contained a transition that confused new volunteers, but it also contained a gesture that explained why two characters trusted each other. Nari suggested keeping the gesture and moving the transition to a later exchange.



Arun asked whether this was the kind of solution that Nari always preferred. She said that the question assumed a stable preference where there was only a method that suited this problem. When the team had to select music the previous week, she had spoken early because she knew the available licences. When the group had to resolve a disagreement about costumes, she had listened first because two designers had information that she lacked. She did not reject the word “quiet”, but she wanted the team to ask what the word described in each situation.



During the review, Mai challenged a line that Nari had drafted. Mai thought the line made the actor sound certain about a decision that was still provisional. Nari thanked her and asked which evidence supported the concern. Mai pointed to the rehearsal log that she had updated after a late change. The log showed that the actor had learned the movement, but not that the audience understood its purpose. Nari replaced “the successful movement” with “the movement tested in rehearsal” and added a question for the next audience trial. The revision made the uncertainty visible rather than hiding it behind a positive adjective.



The team later compared the first audience notes with the plan. Visitors remembered the gesture, but several missed the reason for it. Arun proposed restoring the longer transition. Nari agreed, although she had earlier supported shortening the scene. She explained that the new evidence changed the decision, not her character. A teammate asked whether the label “careful” still fitted her. Nari answered that a label could be a useful shorthand if the examples beside it stayed visible. The board therefore kept a column for observations, a column for interpretations, and a column for decisions that the group might revise.



Before the next meeting, Arun wrote a short instruction: “Bring one point that you observed and one choice that you would change.” The instruction helped people who liked to speak immediately and people who needed time to write. Nari still marked several notes before talking, while Mai still tested ideas aloud. Their different habits did not prevent cooperation. The team used the evidence that each person contributed, checked the assumptions that shaped a decision, and treated a personality description as a prompt for a better question rather than as a final explanation.



### Questions



1. What two details led the team to describe Nari as quiet, and why did she ask the group to read her notes before speaking?

2. In “the notes that she had collected” and “the transition that confused new volunteers”, which relative pronoun can be omitted? Explain the grammatical difference.

3. Why did Nari replace “the successful movement” with “the movement tested in rehearsal”? What uncertainty did the new wording preserve?

4. What did the three board columns help the team separate, and how did the final instruction support different working habits?



**Visible hints before answering:** (1) underline the two early actions Nari takes; (2) identify the verb’s subject: is it already named inside the clause, or is the relative word itself the subject?; (3) compare what the rehearsal log proves with what it does not prove; (4) find the three column names and the two parts of Arun’s instruction.



**Transfer (80–120 words):** Describe a fictional project member using two observable actions. Include one relative clause whose object pronoun can be omitted, one relative clause whose subject pronoun must remain, and one sentence explaining what your description cannot prove. Revise once for punctuation and clear reference.



<details><summary>Hidden key, evidence, and acceptable alternatives</summary>



1. She rarely interrupted and marked suggestions before speaking. She wanted people to read first so that the discussion would begin with the same evidence and each person could choose a focused question. “She preferred written preparation” is acceptable if it is tied to the board notes and not treated as a fixed trait.

2. In “the notes that she had collected”, **that** is the object of **collected**, so it may be omitted: “the notes she had collected”. In “the transition that confused new volunteers”, **that** is the subject of **confused**, so it cannot be omitted; “the transition confused new volunteers” would change the structure. The same subject/object test applies to “the gesture that explained” and “the prop change that the crew had rehearsed”.

3. The movement had been tested by actors, but the log did not prove that the audience understood its purpose. The replacement avoids claiming success and keeps the difference between rehearsal evidence and audience evidence visible.

4. The columns separated observations, interpretations, and decisions open to revision. The instruction asked for one observation and one possible change, allowing immediate speakers and writers who need preparation to contribute. “It gave both groups a concrete entry point” is an acceptable summary.



</details>



## D14 — The rehearsal schedule that moved twice



### Passage (fictional, 592 words)



The student design team had six days to prepare an exhibition about repaired furniture. Its first schedule placed the interview team beside the workshop, where the carpenter demonstrated how damaged chairs were assessed. The group expected a calm week, but the building manager closed the workshop after a water leak. The students moved the interviews to a corridor and began recording the stories of residents who had repaired tables, lamps, and cupboards at home.



Milo, who was coordinating the display, proposed a shorter introduction. He wanted visitors to see objects being repaired rather than read a long explanation first. The introduction written by Sera, however, contained a distinction that the team needed: a chair repaired for safety was not the same as a chair altered for appearance. Milo asked Sera to keep the distinction but move the examples beside the photographs. Sera accepted the change after comparing the two versions. She said that the first draft was not sacred and that a useful edit should leave the reasoning easier to follow.



On the third day, two interviews arrived late. The residents invited to the corridor had misunderstood the revised appointment time, while the resident carrying a box of photographs had been delayed by a bus diversion. Milo did not describe either person as careless. He added a reminder to the message sent the night before and asked the team to record the time at which a change was announced. The reminder was a practical response to a communication gap; it was not evidence that every late arrival had the same cause.



The group then tested three labels for the visitors’ response cards: “patient”, “practical”, and “inventive”. The cards completed during the first hour were longer than the cards completed during the crowded afternoon. Sera pointed out that the label “patient” might describe waiting carefully for a repair demonstration, or it might describe having enough time to write. The team therefore replaced the labels with questions. Visitors could note what they waited for, which decision they made, and how the object changed. The new form produced less tidy totals but more useful descriptions.



Milo’s plan for the final wall used photographs selected by the residents. The images showing loose joints were placed beside the notes explaining how the joints had been tightened. The images showing decorative changes were placed beside comments about colour and use. In one caption, Milo wrote “the lamp restored by Mei”; in another, “the table receiving a new surface”. Sera checked the meanings. The lamp had received a completed action, so **restored** was suitable. The table was shown during an ongoing process, so **receiving** made the timing visible.



On the last morning, the manager reopened the workshop. The team could have returned to its first plan, but the corridor interviews had already changed the exhibition. Milo kept the photographs collected there and added a small map showing the temporary route. He said that the forced change had revealed how residents made decisions in different places. Sera replied that it had also revealed a weakness in their first description of the visitors: the team had tried to name a quality before asking what the visitors actually did.



The exhibition opened with two schedules displayed side by side. One showed the plan made before the leak; the other showed the decisions made after each new observation. The display did not celebrate disruption for its own sake. It showed that a team can adjust a plan, choose a reduced phrase carefully, and keep a claim proportional to the evidence that supports it.



### Questions



1. Why did the team change its visitor labels into questions, and what did it lose by doing so?

2. Explain the difference between “the introduction written by Sera” and “the table receiving a new surface”. Identify the voice and time meaning of each reduced clause.

3. What did the team infer from the late interviews, and what did Milo refuse to infer about the residents?

4. Why were the two schedules displayed together? Give one way this supported the passage’s view of teamwork.



**Visible hints before answering:** (1) compare the first labels with the three replacement questions; (2) ask who performed each participle’s action and whether the action is complete or ongoing; (3) separate what was recorded from the label the team declined to apply; (4) look at what each schedule proves when viewed beside the other.



**Transfer (80–120 words):** Write a fictional exhibition caption containing one passive reduced relative clause and one active **-ing** reduced clause. Add two sentences explaining what the captions show and what they cannot prove about the people involved. Check that each participle has a logical subject.



<details><summary>Hidden key, evidence, and acceptable alternatives</summary>



1. Labels such as “patient” could have different meanings depending on whether someone waited for a demonstration or had time to write. The questions asked what visitors waited for, which decision they made, and how an object changed. The team lost tidy totals or easy comparison, but gained more contextual descriptions. “It traded neat counts for usable explanations” is acceptable.

2. “The introduction written by Sera” is a reduced passive relative clause: Sera wrote it, and the completed draft is being identified. “The table receiving a new surface” is a reduced active clause: the table is shown during an ongoing process. **Written** expresses passive voice; **receiving** expresses active voice and, in this photograph context, an action in progress. The participle alone does not encode past tense or prove completion: the surrounding sentence identifies an existing draft. An **-ing** clause can also describe a repeated action in another context. Neither phrase is an embedded question.

3. They inferred that a communication gap affected the appointments and added a reminder plus a time record. Milo refused to infer that every late resident was careless or had one fixed cause. A response must mention both the practical inference and the rejected personality judgment.

4. The schedules made the original plan and evidence-led revisions visible together. They showed that collaboration involved changing decisions when conditions changed, while preserving the reasons for each change. “The display turned revision into shared evidence” is an acceptable alternative.



</details>



## D15 — The feedback room with a second doorway



### Passage (fictional, 549 words)



The peer-feedback group met in a room that had one large table and two doors. The first door opened onto a busy stairwell; the second opened onto a quiet courtyard. At the first meeting, members chose seats quickly. Some participants faced the stairwell because they wanted to hear people arriving. Others faced the courtyard because they needed fewer interruptions while reading. The facilitator, Linh, wrote “outgoing” beside the first group and “reserved” beside the second, then crossed out both words before the meeting ended.



Linh had asked each member to bring a paragraph that described a difficult collaboration. The paragraph that Tuan submitted concerned a poster designed by four students. Tuan wrote that he had spoken often during early planning, but he had stopped contributing when the group began changing the same sentence without recording the reason. The draft suggested that Tuan was inconsistent. Linh asked him to describe the sequence instead. He explained that he had proposed three layouts, accepted a different layout, and then asked for a single editor when the captions became confusing. His behaviour changed as the task changed.



The paragraph that Mei brought described a different problem. Mei had been the person who checked every citation, but she had overlooked a date in a source list assembled near midnight. She corrected the date the next morning and told the group how the mistake had happened. A member called her “careful”, while another member said that one missed detail proved the label was wrong. Linh asked both members to revise their statements. A useful description had to include the checking habit, the missed date, the correction, and the conditions surrounding the mistake. It did not need to choose between praise and blame.



During the second round, the members used cards that separated observation, interpretation, and next action. The card selected by Tuan asked, “Which change made your contribution less clear?” He answered that unclear ownership had made him repeat decisions. Mei selected a card asking, “When did your checking process become rushed?” She pointed to the late-night source list and added that a shared deadline could have been negotiated earlier. Linh selected a card asking, “Where would a quieter review help?” The group suggested the courtyard for reading and the large table for resolving wording together.



The cards changed the conversation without proving that one room suited everyone. Tuan still enjoyed lively planning when the decision owner was named. Mei still preferred a quiet review for citations but liked explaining corrections at the large table. The group’s notes therefore recorded conditions, choices, results, and proposed adjustments. They used the words “speaks early”, “checks detail”, and “asks for ownership” as temporary search terms. Each term was linked to examples and could be changed after another project.



At the final meeting, Linh placed two drafts beside the doorway. The first was a description full of labels: “outgoing members lead, reserved members check.” The second described actions and conditions: “Members contribute in different ways when ownership, noise, and time pressure change.” The group kept the second draft. It was longer, but it allowed a person to recognise a useful habit without being trapped by a category. The two doors remained open, and members chose where to read, discuss, or revise according to the work in front of them.



### Questions



1. Why did Linh cross out “outgoing” and “reserved”? Use two details from the room or the members’ later actions.

2. In “the paragraph that Tuan submitted” and “the person who checked every citation”, which relative pronoun is an object and which is a subject? State whether either can be omitted.

3. How did the three feedback cards change the discussion? Identify the information each card sought.

4. Compare the two final drafts. Why did the group prefer the longer description, and what limitation did it preserve?



**Visible hints before answering:** (1) compare each label with the concrete behaviour that follows; (2) identify who performed **submitted** and **checked**; (3) match each card to change, time, or place; (4) find the sentence saying that terms could be changed after another project.



**Transfer (80–120 words):** Write a fictional feedback note with one omitted object relative, one subject relative that must remain, and one reduced clause. Describe a behaviour under a named condition, then add a sentence limiting what the observation can prove. Revise for logical participle attachment.



<details><summary>Hidden key, evidence, and acceptable alternatives</summary>



1. The seat choice reflected interruptions and reading needs, not a permanent type. Tuan spoke often in early planning but asked for one editor when ownership became unclear; Mei checked citations carefully but made and corrected a date error under late pressure. Any two details with this context support the answer.

2. In “the paragraph that Tuan submitted”, **that** is the object of **submitted** and can be omitted: “the paragraph Tuan submitted”. In “the person who checked every citation”, **who** is the subject of **checked** and must remain. “The person checked every citation” changes the structure and does not identify the person in the intended way.

3. Tuan’s card sought the change that made contribution unclear; Mei’s asked when checking became rushed; Linh’s asked where a quieter review would help. Together they moved the group from labels to a concrete change, time condition, place, and next action.

4. The longer draft named actions and conditions instead of treating categories as predictions. It preserved the limitation that the temporary search terms could change after another project; it did not claim that one room or one description suited everyone.



</details>



## Cycle-wide language check



For each day, mark one full subject relative and one full object relative, rewrite the object relative with its pronoun omitted, and identify one reduced clause. The passages do not promise that an omitted object relative already appears in each day’s text. Explain whether the relative word is a subject or object before omitting it. For reduced clauses, name active or passive voice and whether the context presents an ongoing or completed action. Do not label the clauses beginning **when**, **where**, or **why** as relatives unless they modify an antecedent noun; in this cycle, those words may introduce time, place, or reason information. Accept a semantically equivalent answer when the grammatical relation and passage evidence remain accurate.



## Source and boundary note



The passages, questions, keys, hints, and transfers are original course writing. The people, projects, and observations are fictional; they make no medical, CEFR, diagnostic, MBTI, Big Five, or empirical personality claim. Grammar explanations are bounded to the retained Grammar 4 file linked above. This author check does not certify independent acceptance, course completion, test readiness, or learner attainment.


<a id="sec-ielts-phase-3-writing-cycle4-writing-md"></a>
## Source 028 — `IELTS/Phase-3/writing/cycle4-writing.md`

# Writing — Cycle 4: omission and reduced clauses in evidence notes

Chu kỳ này luyện bỏ đại từ quan hệ làm tân ngữ và dùng mệnh đề quan hệ rút gọn trong ba văn bản hư cấu về nhóm làm việc. Mỗi bài yêu cầu **200–250 từ**. Dành 50 phút: đọc đề và lập ý (8), viết nháp (15), kiểm tra chủ thể/tân ngữ (10), sửa câu (10), tự chấm (7). Nhân vật và quan sát chỉ là hư cấu; không chẩn đoán, dự báo hay khẳng định khoa học về tính cách.

## Quy tắc chấm 0/1/2

Viết bài tiếng Anh trước khi mở mẫu. Với từng tiêu chí: 2 điểm nếu đáp ứng đầy đủ và chính xác, 1 nếu đúng một phần hoặc còn lỗi nhỏ, 0 nếu thiếu hoặc sai nghĩa. Tổng 12 điểm dùng để chọn phần cần sửa, không chứng nhận trình độ. Giữ bản nháp và bản sửa, rồi giải thích một thay đổi cụ thể.

## D13 — Biên bản bàn giao ở thư viện

### Nhiệm vụ

Viết một biên bản 200–250 từ cho trưởng nhóm thư viện hư cấu sau một lần bàn giao ca bị thiếu thông tin. Nêu hai quan sát cụ thể, giải thích một quy trình mới, và kết thúc bằng giới hạn của biên bản. Dùng một mệnh đề quan hệ có thể bỏ đại từ tân ngữ (ví dụ *the list I checked*) và một mệnh đề có chủ ngữ quan hệ phải giữ lại (ví dụ *the volunteer who opened the cabinet*). Không biến một sự cố thành nhãn tính cách cố định.

### Quy trình và tự kiểm

1. Lập bốn ý: điều xảy ra, điều kiện, thông tin thiếu, quy trình mới.
2. Gạch chân động từ sau **who/that/which** để xác định đại từ là chủ ngữ hay tân ngữ trước khi bỏ.
3. Sửa bản nháp: thử bỏ đại từ ở mệnh đề tân ngữ, giữ đại từ ở mệnh đề chủ ngữ, rồi kiểm tra giới hạn diễn giải.

**Gợi ý tăng dần (hiển thị trước đáp án):** (1) đặt một chủ ngữ khác sau **that** để nhận ra tân ngữ; (2) hỏi ai thực hiện động từ trong mệnh đề *who opened*; (3) viết lại câu cuối bằng “This record does not prove…”.

**Chuyển giao và tự chấm:** Viết thêm một câu hướng dẫn người nhận ca. Chấm 0–2 cho nhiệm vụ/độ dài, bằng chứng, bỏ đúng tân ngữ, giữ đúng chủ ngữ, quy trình, và giới hạn/sửa lỗi (12 điểm). Ghi lại một câu trước và sau khi sửa.

<details><summary>Mẫu gốc, chú giải và tiêu chí đầy đủ</summary>

### Model (247 words)

Library handover record

During the afternoon shift, Mai left the reception desk to guide a group of pupils to the archive cabinet. She told a volunteer that she would return, but the list Mai checked did not identify the person taking the desk. A visitor waited while two volunteers searched for the key. The incident showed a gap in the handover process; it did not prove that Mai was always careless.

The team should add a receiver column to the list that every shift uses. The person leaving a station must name the receiver, and the volunteer who opens the cabinet must repeat the assignment. This makes temporary responsibility visible. The checklist, which confused the first team, should also be read aloud before the next group arrives. The receiver can ask about the checklist before taking responsibility, and the team can record any uncertainty beside the task.

For the next two weeks, the shift leader should record the handover time and any question that remains unclear. The record will help the team find a procedural gap and revise the process. It will not show how Mai would act in every library or under every demand. Further observations are needed before anyone makes a wider description.

The revised form gives the team a practical routine: name the receiver, repeat the task, and write down what remains uncertain. It also gives Mai an opportunity to correct the record when a future handover contains information that the first note missed.

### Chú giải và sửa câu

- **the list that Mai checked** có **that** là tân ngữ của *checked*, nên có thể viết **the list Mai checked**.
- **the volunteer who opens the cabinet** có **who** là chủ ngữ của *opens*, nên không thể bỏ.
- Trong **The checklist, which confused the first team, …**, **which** bổ nghĩa cho **checklist**, không phải toàn bộ hành động sửa. Đây là thông tin thêm nên dùng dấu phẩy. Bản trước **the list that Mai checked** có thể sửa thành **the list Mai checked**: bỏ đúng tân ngữ mà không đổi nghĩa.

### Rubric và đáp án thay thế (12 điểm)

- 0–2: biên bản đúng 200–250 từ, đủ sự cố, quy trình và giới hạn.
- 0–2: hai quan sát gắn với điều kiện hoặc thời điểm.
- 0–2: bỏ đúng đại từ làm tân ngữ.
- 0–2: giữ đúng đại từ làm chủ ngữ.
- 0–2: quy trình mới cụ thể và khả thi.
- 0–2: sửa câu có giải thích và không gán nhãn cố định.

Chấp nhận *the list I checked* hoặc *the list that I checked*. Chấp nhận bất kỳ quy trình bàn giao hợp lý nào nếu nêu rõ người nhận và bước nhắc lại. Không chấp nhận bỏ **who** trong mệnh đề mà **who** là người thực hiện động từ.
</details>

## D14 — Báo cáo chỉnh sửa nhãn trưng bày

### Nhiệm vụ

Viết một báo cáo 200–250 từ cho nhóm trưng bày hư cấu. Mô tả hai thay đổi của một hoặc nhiều vật hoặc bản thảo, rồi đề xuất cách ghi chú để không nhầm hành động chủ động và bị động. Dùng một mệnh đề rút gọn chủ động **-ing** bổ nghĩa đúng danh từ và một mệnh đề rút gọn bị động **-ed** bổ nghĩa đúng danh từ. Nêu rõ bằng chứng cho thấy hành động diễn ra trong bối cảnh nào; không tự suy ra thì hoặc mức hoàn tất chỉ từ hình thức phân từ.

### Quy trình và tự kiểm

1. Lập bảng nhỏ: danh từ được bổ nghĩa, ai thực hiện hành động, và hành động nhận tác động.
2. Viết một câu kiểu *the editor checking the labels* và một câu kiểu *the labels checked by the editor*.
3. Sửa: gạch mũi tên từ phân từ về danh từ, rồi viết giới hạn “The form alone does not establish…” nếu cần.

**Gợi ý tăng dần (hiển thị trước đáp án):** (1) hỏi danh từ nào đang làm hành động trong **checking**; (2) hỏi danh từ nào nhận hành động trong **checked by**; (3) dùng trạng từ thời gian trong câu chính để diễn đạt thời điểm, không bắt phân từ tự mang thì.

**Chuyển giao và tự chấm:** Thêm một chú thích thay thế cho vật trưng bày. Chấm 0–2 cho nhiệm vụ/độ dài, bằng chứng, chủ thể **-ing**, quan hệ bị động **-ed**, giải thích thời gian/điều kiện, và sửa/giới hạn (12 điểm). Ghi một câu trước và sau khi kiểm tra mũi tên chủ thể.

<details><summary>Mẫu gốc, chú giải và tiêu chí đầy đủ</summary>

### Model (242 words)

Exhibition-label revision report

The team revised a display label for a table repaired by a local family. The first draft said “the table repairing by the family”, but that phrase incorrectly made the table the agent. The team changed it to “the table repaired by the family”, a passive reduced relative that identifies a table receiving the repair. The label then included “the editor checking the dates asked for one clearer source.” Here the active reduced relative modifies editor, who performs the checking.

During the meeting, the editor read notes collected from the family. She noticed that the table had been strengthened for safety, while a second table had been painted to change its appearance. The group kept both explanations because they described different decisions. The reduced forms make the labels concise, but the forms alone do not establish an absolute time or prove that an action is complete. The surrounding sentence and context supply that information: “Yesterday, the editor checked the dates” and “Tomorrow, the editor will check the dates.”

The report recommends recording the agent, the object receiving an action, and the date of observation. This record helps visitors understand each label without turning an object or a person into a permanent category. It also reminds the team that a shorter clause must preserve the evidence’s meaning. A second editor will read the next draft before printing, so an unclear attachment can be corrected while the label is still provisional.

### Chú giải và sửa câu

- **the table repaired by the family** là bị động; **the table** nhận hành động.
- **the editor checking the dates** là chủ động; **the editor** thực hiện hành động. Bản sai **the table repairing by the family** được sửa thành **the table repaired by the family** để chiếc bàn nhận hành động thay vì làm tác nhân.
- *Checking* và *repaired* không tự mã hóa toàn bộ thì hoặc mức hoàn tất; câu chính và ngữ cảnh cung cấp thông tin đó.

### Rubric và đáp án thay thế (12 điểm)

- 0–2: báo cáo đúng 200–250 từ, có vật, thay đổi và đề xuất.
- 0–2: bằng chứng phân biệt sửa vì an toàn và sửa vì vẻ ngoài.
- 0–2: **-ing** gắn với danh từ thực hiện hành động.
- 0–2: **-ed** gắn với danh từ nhận hành động, có thể nêu tác nhân.
- 0–2: giải thích thời gian dựa vào câu chính/ngữ cảnh.
- 0–2: sửa câu có mũi tên chủ thể và giới hạn đúng.

Chấp nhận *the family-repaired table* chỉ khi cấu trúc và nghĩa vẫn rõ, nhưng câu đầy đủ với **repaired by** là lựa chọn an toàn. Chấp nhận các động từ khác nếu xác định đúng chủ thể và đối tượng. Không chấm đúng câu khiến vật trở thành tác nhân ngoài ý nghĩa hư cấu đã nêu.
</details>

## D15 — Nhật ký phản hồi sau một lần họp

### Nhiệm vụ

Viết một nhật ký phản hồi 200–250 từ về một cuộc họp nhóm hư cấu. So sánh một hành động trong lúc họp với một hành động sau khi họp, rồi đề xuất một bước tiếp theo. Dùng một mệnh đề rút gọn chủ động và một mệnh đề rút gọn bị động, nhưng phải phân biệt chúng với mệnh đề trạng ngữ mở đầu như “Reviewing the notes, the facilitator…”. Dùng một mệnh đề quan hệ đầy đủ mà đại từ chủ ngữ phải giữ lại. Kết thúc bằng giới hạn về diễn giải.

### Quy trình và tự kiểm

1. Lập hai cột “trong họp/sau họp”, ghi người thực hiện và người/vật nhận hành động.
2. Viết một mệnh đề đầy đủ với **who** làm chủ ngữ; không bỏ **who** chỉ vì muốn câu ngắn.
3. Sửa: kiểm tra mệnh đề **-ing** đứng sau danh từ có bổ nghĩa cho danh từ đó hay là trạng ngữ mở đầu; kiểm tra mệnh đề bị động có danh từ nhận hành động.

**Gợi ý tăng dần (hiển thị trước đáp án):** (1) hỏi “ai đang xem xét ghi chú?” trong câu mở đầu; (2) tìm danh từ đứng trước **written/selected**; (3) viết câu giới hạn rằng một cuộc họp không giải thích mọi phản ứng sau này.

**Chuyển giao và tự chấm:** Viết lại câu có **Reviewing the notes, the facilitator…** bằng **While the facilitator was reviewing…** để chứng minh nghĩa không đổi. Chấm 0–2 cho nhiệm vụ/độ dài, bằng chứng, chủ ngữ quan hệ được giữ, phân biệt hai kiểu rút gọn, bước tiếp theo, và giới hạn/sửa câu (12 điểm). Ghi lý do câu mở đầu là trạng ngữ hay quan hệ rút gọn.

<details><summary>Mẫu gốc, chú giải và tiêu chí đầy đủ</summary>

### Model (220 words)

Feedback journal after a group meeting

During the meeting, Tuan asked the group to name one person responsible for the draft. The colleague who recorded the decision wrote the name on the board, so everyone knew who would check the next version. After the meeting, Tuan sent a specific question instead of rewriting the whole document. The group kept the examples selected by the team and removed one comment that was too general. Those examples included a practical deadline question and a request to check the wording with the original writer.

The facilitator reviewing the notes noticed a missing deadline. Reviewing the notes, the facilitator then asked the writer to add a date. Tuan had waited for the group’s decision before suggesting a timetable. The writer found a specific question easier to answer than a general comment. They agreed to send everyone the same version.

The next step is for the responsible person to record a deadline and invite the writer to check the question before editing. The journal shows clearer responsibility in this meeting. It does not prove that Tuan is always cautious, that the facilitator will always detect an omission, or that the revised routine will solve every later meeting. The group should keep another record and compare the next discussion with this one before extending the description.

### Chú giải và sửa câu

- **who recorded the decision** là mệnh đề đầy đủ, **who** làm chủ ngữ nên phải giữ.
- **selected by the team** là bị động rút gọn bổ nghĩa cho **examples**.
- **the facilitator reviewing the notes** là active reduced relative bổ nghĩa cho **facilitator**. **Reviewing the notes, the facilitator …** là trạng ngữ phân từ; chủ thể ngầm cũng là **facilitator**. Bản sai **Reviewing the notes, the deadline was missing** gắn sai chủ thể; sửa thành **Reviewing the notes, the facilitator noticed a missing deadline**.

### Rubric và đáp án thay thế (12 điểm)

- 0–2: nhật ký đúng 200–250 từ, đủ so sánh và bước tiếp theo.
- 0–2: hai hành động gắn với thời điểm trong/sau họp.
- 0–2: giữ đúng **who** làm chủ ngữ.
- 0–2: phân biệt trạng ngữ mở đầu với reduced relative chủ động/bị động.
- 0–2: bước tiếp theo cụ thể và khả thi.
