- 0–2: giới hạn và sửa câu giải thích đúng nghĩa.

Chấp nhận mệnh đề mở đầu khác nếu chủ thể ngầm rõ và có thể viết lại bằng *while/when*. Chấp nhận reduced relative khác nếu nó bổ nghĩa đúng danh từ. Không chấp nhận bỏ **who** khi **who** là người thực hiện động từ.
</details>

## Boundary note

Ba nhiệm vụ, mẫu, chú giải và rubric là nội dung gốc của khóa học. Nhân vật, nhóm và quan sát là hư cấu. File này không đưa ra khẳng định chẩn đoán, khoa học, CEFR, band score, thành tích người học hoặc độ sẵn sàng khóa học. Đây là author-side writing resource chờ independent review.


<a id="sec-ielts-phase-3-speaking-cycle4-speaking-md"></a>
## Source 029 — `IELTS/Phase-3/speaking/cycle4-speaking.md`

# Speaking — Cycle 4: omission, reduced clauses, and clear attachment

Đây là ba buổi D13–D15. Dùng vocabulary core hiện hành và các quy tắc trong [omission and reduced clauses](#sec-ielts-phase-3-grammar-04-omission-reduced-clauses-md) cùng [Cycle 4 vocabulary](#sec-ielts-phase-3-vocabulary-04-personality-cycle4-md). Không thêm core vocabulary mới. Mỗi bài nói về nhân vật hư cấu; không chẩn đoán hoặc gán nhãn người thật.

## Quy trình đúng 50 phút cho mỗi buổi

1. **Phút 0–8:** đọc prompt, chọn 2–3 chi tiết có nghĩa và lập dàn ý ba gạch đầu dòng.
2. **Phút 8–18:** chuẩn bị đúng **1 phút**, nói **60–90 giây**, rồi nghe bản ghi của chính mình và ghi một chỗ khó hiểu.
3. **Phút 18–28:** chọn đúng **hai lỗi** có thể sửa; ghi câu trước và câu sau.
4. **Phút 28–40:** chuẩn bị lại đúng **1 phút**, nói lại **60–90 giây**, nghe lại và ghi một thay đổi có ích.
5. **Phút 40–50:** tự chấm 10 điểm, điền error log và chọn bước tiếp theo.

**Rubric 10 điểm dùng cho mọi buổi:** prompt và 2–3 chi tiết (0–3: 0 bỏ prompt; 1 có ý nhưng quá ít chi tiết; 2 đủ prompt và hai chi tiết; 3 chi tiết cụ thể nối với lý do); mệnh đề/antecedent và ý nghĩa (0–3: 0 không có hoặc sai nghĩa; 1 có nhưng antecedent/attachment mơ hồ; 2 hầu hết đúng; 3 đúng và giải thích được quan hệ); từ core đã học dùng đúng ngữ cảnh (0–2: 0 không có cách dùng phù hợp; 1 có từ phù hợp nhưng còn lỗi hoặc thiếu ngữ cảnh; 2 dùng chính xác và tự nhiên); dễ hiểu và tự sửa (0–2: 0 không hoàn tất; 1 hoàn tất nhưng chưa sửa rõ; 2 bản thu lại rõ hơn). **7–10:** sang buổi tiếp; **5–6:** sửa hai lỗi và thu lại một lần; **dưới 5:** dùng dàn ý ba câu, luyện câu mẫu, rồi nói lại. Accent không bị trừ điểm; chỉ ghi lỗi làm người nghe khó hiểu.

**Error log:** `Ngày/buổi:` · `Prompt:` · `Lỗi 1 và câu trước:` · `Sửa 1:` · `Lỗi 2 và câu trước:` · `Sửa 2:` · `Thay đổi nghe rõ hơn trong bản thu lại:` · `Điểm/10 và bước tiếp theo:`

Nhân vật và nơi chốn là hư cấu. Có thể thay bằng trải nghiệm cá nhân hoặc giữ hoàn toàn hư cấu; không cần nói thông tin riêng tư. Các bài mẫu là spoken English tự nhiên, không phải bài giảng ngữ pháp.

## D13 — A fictional repair note with an omitted object relative

**Prompt:** Describe a fictional learner who repaired a short workshop note. Include 2–3 meaningful details: the note selected, one person who checked it, and one fair next step. Use an object relative that can be omitted and retain a subject relative that cannot. Speak for 60–90 seconds (120–180 words).

<details><summary>Gợi ý 1 — chọn ý</summary>

chọn một note mà một người đã sửa; thêm ai kiểm tra và điều kiện của bước tiếp theo

</details>
<details><summary>Gợi ý 2 — kiểm tra cấu trúc</summary>

trong “the note I revised”, I là chủ thể nên đại từ là object và có thể bỏ; trong “the learner who checked it”, who là subject nên không bỏ

</details>
<details><summary>Gợi ý 3 — giới hạn diễn giải</summary>

một note tốt ở một buổi không chứng minh người học luôn cẩn thận; nói rõ giới hạn của quan sát

</details>

<details><summary>Full model (delayed)</summary>

**Natural spoken model (146 words):**

“My fictional learner Lan repaired a short workshop note before the group shared it. The note I revised described a borrowed projector, and I kept the original detail so the next volunteer could check the equipment. Lan, who checked the final list beside me, noticed that one sentence named a person but did not explain the task. She asked the coordinator to add the missing action instead of guessing what the person intended. I would give Lan the note that needs a second check, because the task requires careful reading and a clear record. The note she revised became easier to follow, but this single revision does not prove that Lan is always precise. At the next meeting, the group could compare two notes written under different time limits. That comparison would help us discuss the work without turning one observation into a permanent personality label.”

</details>

**Actual clause and meaning check:** “The note I revised” omits an object relative: I is the subject of revised and the note is its object. “Lan, who checked the final list beside me,” is non-defining information about named Lan, with a clear antecedent. “the learner who checked” would keep who because who is the subject. The final comparison is a limit on the fictional observation.

**Worked before/after correction:** Before: “The learner checked the note was careful.” After: “The learner who checked the note was careful.” Here who is the subject and cannot be omitted. A separate valid object-relative sentence is “The note I checked was clear.”
## D14 — A fictional profile with active and passive reductions

**Prompt:** Explain how a fictional study group used a profile during a planning meeting. Include 2–3 meaningful details: a person actively reviewing, a profile written by someone else, and a cautious next step. Use one active reduced relative and one passive reduced relative. Speak for 60–90 seconds (120–180 words).

<details><summary>Gợi ý 1 — chọn ý</summary>

đặt một người đang thực hiện hành động cạnh danh từ; đặt một profile nhận hành động written cạnh danh từ

</details>
<details><summary>Gợi ý 2 — kiểm tra cấu trúc</summary>

active: “the member reviewing the profile”; passive: “the profile written by Mai”; không dùng V-ing cho danh từ nhận hành động

</details>
<details><summary>Gợi ý 3 — giới hạn diễn giải</summary>

phân biệt hành động nhìn thấy trong cuộc họp với kết luận rộng hơn về tính cách

</details>

<details><summary>Full model (delayed)</summary>

**Natural spoken model (153 words):**

“Our fictional study group used a profile to plan a small discussion. The member reviewing the profile, Jo, marked two sentences that sounded too certain about another learner. A profile written by Mai included examples from one afternoon, so Jo asked the group to name the conditions before choosing a description. The tutor suggested a short question: what did the learner do, and what else could explain it? I would keep the profile beside the meeting notes and invite Mai to revise one sentence after hearing the learner’s view. Jo compared the wording with a note from the meeting and circled one claim that lacked an example. Mai agreed to ask a follow-up question before sharing the next draft. Those details support a careful edit, but they do not prove that any fictional learner has a fixed trait. A later meeting could test whether the revised wording remains clear when the task changes.”

</details>

**Actual clause and meaning check:** “the member reviewing the profile” is active: the member performs reviewing. “a profile written by Mai” is passive: the profile receives the writing. The noun immediately before each reduced clause supplies the intended antecedent, and the meeting context keeps the attachment clear.

**Worked before/after correction:** Before: “The profile writing by Mai was cautious.” After: “The profile written by Mai was cautious.” Mai wrote the profile, so the profile needs the passive V-ed form. For active meaning, use “the member reviewing the profile,” not “the member reviewed by the profile.”
## D15 — A fictional team plan with attachment choices

**Prompt:** Describe a fictional team choosing a plan. Include 2–3 meaningful details: one noun followed by an active reduced relative, one noun followed by a passive reduced relative, and one fronted adverbial participle with the correct subject. After recording, explain separately how you avoid a dangling or ambiguous attachment. Speak for 60–90 seconds (120–180 words).

<details><summary>Gợi ý 1 — chọn ý</summary>

chọn a volunteer carrying the map và a plan approved by the group; sau đó nói ai đã review the plan

</details>
<details><summary>Gợi ý 2 — kiểm tra cấu trúc</summary>

“The volunteer carrying the map…” modifies volunteer; “the plan approved by the group…” modifies plan; “After reviewing the plan, I…” makes I the subject

</details>
<details><summary>Gợi ý 3 — giới hạn diễn giải</summary>

với cụm phân từ ở đầu câu, chủ ngữ mệnh đề chính phải là chủ thể ngữ nghĩa phù hợp của cụm đó (có thể là người hoặc vật, tùy cấu trúc); giữ giới hạn cho quyết định hư cấu

</details>

<details><summary>Full model (delayed)</summary>

**Natural spoken model (146 words):**

“The fictional team chose a route for a community noticeboard. The volunteer carrying the map pointed to a quieter entrance, while the plan approved by the group kept the public timetable unchanged. After reviewing the plan, I asked whether the entrance would still be open during the short event. The team did not treat one suggestion as proof that the volunteer preferred quiet places; we recorded the time, the map, and the reason for the choice. I would ask the volunteer to explain the route again and invite another member to check the signs. We then compared the map with the timetable and found one sign that needed a clearer arrow. The volunteer agreed to check that sign with Jo before anyone followed the route. A second meeting could reveal whether the signs work under different conditions, but this fictional meeting alone cannot settle that question.”

</details>

**Actual clause and meaning check:** “the volunteer carrying the map” is an active reduced relative: volunteer carries map. “the plan approved by the group” is passive: group approved plan. In “After reviewing the plan, I asked…”, I is the reviewer; the fronted adverbial is not a reduced relative and does not attach to the plan.

**Worked before/after correction:** Before: “After reviewing the plan, the entrance seemed confusing.” After: “After reviewing the plan, I found the entrance confusing.” The second version gives the reviewing action a human subject and removes the dangling modifier. “The signs placed by Jo…” would be a separate passive reduced relative.


<a id="sec-ielts-phase-3-reading-cycle5-reading-md"></a>
## Source 030 — `IELTS/Phase-3/reading/cycle5-reading.md`

# Reading — Cycle 5: an integrated description workshop

This cycle covers D17–D19 through three original fictional passages about teams that describe behaviour carefully. Each passage combines defining and non-defining relatives, **whose**, **where**, **when**, and reduced clauses. The descriptions are evidence-led but never diagnostic or predictive. Spend about 50 minutes per day: preview and glossary (5), first reading (12), four questions (15), evidence check and hints (10), and transfer writing (8). Try the questions before opening each hidden key. Grammar reference: [Grammar 5 — Personality description workshop](#sec-ielts-phase-3-grammar-05-personality-description-workshop-md).


## Cách học và tự kiểm tra

Dành khoảng50phút mỗi ngày: xem tiêu đề và từ khó5phút, đọc lần đầu12phút, trả lời bốn câu15phút, đối chiếu bằng chứng và gợi ý10phút, viết đoạn chuyển giao8phút. Làm câu hỏi trước khi mở đáp án. Với mỗi relative clause, xác định danh từ được bổ nghĩa, vai trò chủ ngữ/tân ngữ và dấu phẩy. Không gọi mọi mệnh đề có **when/where/why** là relative clause: chúng phải bổ nghĩa cho một danh từ. Tự chấm mỗi câu0–2:2đúng cả ý và bằng chứng hoặc giải thích ngữ pháp,1đúng một phần,0sai/chưa trả lời; tổng8điểm dùng để tìm phần cần sửa, không chứng nhận trình độ. Sửa ít nhất một câu và viết đoạn80–120từ trước khi chuyển ngày.

## D17 — The profile assembled from small decisions

### Passage (fictional, 517 words)

The archive team needed a short profile of its new volunteer, Hana, who had joined during a crowded month. The coordinator, Pavel, wanted a paragraph that would help colleagues work with her. His first draft called Hana “independent, organised, and introverted”. Hana read the paragraph beside the filing cabinet where the team stored unfinished descriptions. She agreed that the words were familiar, but asked Pavel to show the observations that supported them.

Pavel opened the notes that he had collected during three shifts. On Monday, Hana had arrived early and labelled boxes that lacked dates. On Wednesday, she had asked another volunteer to check a list that she had sorted. On Friday, she had moved to the quieter table when the public tour began, then returned to the shared table when the team needed to compare names. The notes showed choices that changed with the task. They did not show a single quality that explained every choice.

Hana suggested a different profile. “Hana checks the condition of each box before entering a date. She asks for a second check when a list will affect another person. During a public tour, she works at the courtyard table, which has fewer interruptions. When the tour ends, she joins the group that compares uncertain names.” Pavel noticed that the new version was longer, but each sentence answered a practical question. The relative clause beginning **which** described the courtyard table, not Hana, and the clause beginning **that** identified the group she joined.

The team then discussed a note that Hana had written about a missing photograph. The photograph, whose envelope had no date, was placed in a temporary folder. Hana did not claim that the image belonged to a particular year. She wrote that its location was known, its date was uncertain, and the next action was to compare the handwriting with two dated envelopes. Pavel liked the possessive clause because it connected the envelope with its missing date without pretending that the date had been discovered.

A colleague, Tomas, said that the profile sounded cautious because it used too many details. Hana replied that a shorter label could be useful as a search term, provided the examples remained nearby. She proposed a heading, “checks conditions before deciding”, followed by three notes. The heading was a temporary summary; the notes were the evidence that could confirm, refine, or challenge it. The team accepted the structure and added a line explaining that behaviour observed during a busy tour might differ from behaviour observed during quiet sorting.

At the end of the week, Pavel asked whether Hana now considered herself organised. Hana said that she could recognise a repeated practice in the notes, but she would not use one week to explain all future work. The team’s final profile therefore described actions, conditions, and possible support. It did not turn a person into a list of permanent types. The paragraph was useful because a colleague could act on it: provide a second checker for uncertain records, offer a quiet table for focused sorting, and ask before assuming why a choice had been made.

### Questions

1. Why did Hana ask Pavel to show the observations behind the three labels? Give two examples of choices that changed with the task.
2. In “the notes that he had collected” and “the courtyard table, which has fewer interruptions”, identify the antecedent and grammatical role of each relative word. Which clause is non-defining?
3. What does “whose envelope had no date” express, and why did Hana avoid claiming that the photograph’s year was known?
4. How did the final profile turn description into practical support without claiming to explain Hana’s future behaviour?

**Visible hints before answering:** (1) find the three dated shifts and compare the setting; (2) identify the noun before each relative clause and ask who performed the verb; (3) look at the noun immediately after **whose** and the evidence Hana says is still uncertain; (4) list the support actions in the final paragraph and the sentence that limits prediction.

**Transfer (80–120 words):** Write a fictional profile with one defining object relative, one non-defining relative with commas, and one **whose** clause. Add two observable actions under different conditions and one sentence stating what the profile cannot prove. Check that every relative clause attaches to the intended noun.

<details><summary>Hidden key, evidence, and acceptable alternatives</summary>

1. Hana wanted the labels to remain accountable to observable evidence. She moved to a quieter table during the tour but returned to the shared table for name comparison; she asked for a second check on a consequential list while sorting boxes independently. Any two task-linked examples are acceptable.
2. In “the notes that he had collected”, **that** refers to **notes** and is the object of **collected**, so it can be omitted. In “the courtyard table, which has fewer interruptions”, **which** refers to **table** and is the subject of **has**; the commas mark a non-defining clause, which adds information and cannot be reduced to an omitted subject pronoun here.
3. **Whose envelope had no date** links the photograph to an envelope and identifies the envelope’s missing date. Hana avoided a year claim because the note established location and uncertainty only; she planned to compare handwriting rather than infer a date from an undated envelope.
4. It gave colleagues concrete actions—use a second checker, offer a quiet table, and ask before interpreting a choice—while describing the conditions under which the observations occurred. The temporary heading could be refined, and the profile explicitly refused to explain all future work.

</details>

## D18 — The workshop map with a missing arrow

### Passage (fictional, 505 words)

The language club designed a workshop for volunteers who often gave feedback on short stories. Its first map showed a straight route: read the story, mark the strongest paragraph, and suggest a revision. The route looked efficient, but it ignored the writers who needed to understand a question before choosing a revision. Mira, whose job was to coordinate the workshop, asked the volunteers to test the map with a fictional draft about a bus station.

The draft followed a character who waited beside a timetable that displayed two different departure times. One volunteer, Ellis, marked the opening as confusing. Another volunteer, Noor, wrote that the confusion was deliberate because the character had received two messages. Mira asked the group to identify the evidence before choosing a label. The paragraph that Ellis had marked contained the timetable; the message that Noor had noticed appeared three lines later. Neither observation alone explained the whole scene.

Mira changed the map. The first step became “name the sentence that created your question”. The second asked readers to find the detail that answered or complicated it. The third invited the writer to choose between two revisions, one that clarified the timetable and another that made the conflicting messages more visible. The volunteer who preferred a quick judgement had to record a reason. The volunteer who needed time could write before the discussion began.

During the trial, a writer called Samira chose the revision that made the messages more visible. She explained that the character’s uncertainty was part of the story, but the reader needed to know why the timetable mattered. Mira noted that the choice was made after the evidence had been compared, not because Samira had been assigned a personality type. The revision suggested a next action: add one phrase before the second message. It did not prove that Samira always preferred ambiguity.

The volunteers then examined the language on their own feedback cards. One card said, “The sentence receiving the most questions needs work.” Mira changed it to “The sentence that received the most questions needs a closer look.” The first version could suggest an action in progress, although the questions had already been counted. The second version made the completed event clearer. Another card read, “The reader waiting at the station notices the sign.” Mira kept the **-ing** clause because the reader was shown during an ongoing wait, but she checked that the reader, not the sign, was doing the waiting.

At the end of the session, the club placed the old map beside the revised map. The old route was not useless: it reminded readers to notice structure. The revised route added a question, an evidence check, and a choice that the writer could explain. Mira wrote a note whose purpose was to protect this sequence: “A useful description says what happened, where the evidence appears, and when an interpretation remains uncertain.” The note did not make every reader work in the same way. It made the reasoning visible enough for different readers to join it.

### Questions

1. What was missing from the workshop’s original straight route, and how did the revised map support both quick and careful readers?
2. Compare “the paragraph that Ellis had marked” with “the sentence receiving the most questions”. Which is a full relative clause, and what is the voice/time meaning of the reduced clause?
3. Why did Mira change the feedback card from the reduced “receiving” phrase to the full clause “that received”? What did she check in the other **-ing** example?
4. What was the purpose of the note “whose purpose was to protect this sequence”, and how did the two maps work together?

**Visible hints before answering:** (1) locate the three new map steps and the two kinds of reader; (2) ask whether the relative word is followed by a full subject-and-verb structure; (3) compare the time of counting with the time of writing the card; (4) match the note to “what, where, when” and compare the purposes of both maps.

**Transfer (80–120 words):** Design a three-step feedback route. Include one full relative clause, one passive reduced clause, and one active **-ing** clause with a clear subject. Add a **whose** clause explaining the purpose of one step and state one uncertainty that the route cannot resolve.

<details><summary>Hidden key, evidence, and acceptable alternatives</summary>

1. The straight route had no step for naming a question, finding the evidence that answered or complicated it, or explaining a revision choice. The revised map required a reason for quick judgements and allowed a writer who needed time to write before discussion. “It supported different pacing while keeping the evidence check” is acceptable.
2. “The paragraph that Ellis had marked” is a full relative clause; **that** is the object of **had marked**. “The sentence receiving the most questions” is a reduced active clause, with **receiving** used here for a relation during the question period. An **-ing** clause can also have a habitual meaning in another context. Mira replaced it with the full clause **that received the most questions** to state the counted past event explicitly; **received** is a finite simple-past verb in this full active clause, not a passive reduced participle.
3. The full clause **that received** uses a finite past-tense verb to match the counted event. It makes the timing explicit rather than relying on the reduced phrase. In “the reader waiting at the station”, Mira checked that **reader** was the logical subject of **waiting**, not the sign, so the reduced clause did not dangle.
4. The note preserved the sequence of event, evidence location, and uncertainty in an interpretation. The old map highlighted structure, while the revised map made reasoning and choice visible; together they retained a useful starting point and added an accountable process.

</details>

## D19 — The final description beside the open door

### Passage (fictional, 574 words)

For the final workshop task, the group had to write a 200-word description of a fictional discussion leader. The brief asked for evidence, connections between ideas, and a careful limitation. It did not ask writers to choose a personality type. The model paragraph, which sat beside the open door, described a leader who changed her questions when the group’s needs changed. The facilitator, Jo, asked everyone to inspect the grammar before copying the structure.

Leila chose to describe Noor, who led a neighbourhood reading circle. Noor prepared a list of questions that invited examples, but she removed two questions when the meeting moved from a large room to a narrow café. The café, whose tables were close together, made it difficult for several people to spread out their notes. Noor therefore asked readers to compare one passage at a time. She did not conclude that the group had become less interested; she recorded that the setting had changed the available space and time.

Leila’s first draft included this sentence: “Noor, who the members trusted, asked for examples, and the notes written during the meeting were kept.” Jo asked Leila to check the attachment. The non-defining clause **who the members trusted** correctly added information about Noor. The reduced passive clause **written during the meeting** correctly described notes that members wrote. However, the sentence joined two ideas with a comma and **and** without showing how the notes related to the request. Leila split it into two sentences and added the reason in the next line.

The revised passage said that Noor asked for examples before accepting a general comment. A member, who had initially called the discussion “unproductive”, pointed to a page whose margin contained three concrete questions. The member changed the comment after noticing that the group had produced useful questions even though it had not reached a final decision. Leila wrote that the example supported a claim about that meeting. It did not prove that Noor’s method would work in every group.

Jo then asked Leila to include one reduced active clause. Leila wrote, “The leader reviewing the final notes invited the members to choose one question for next week.” Jo checked the attachment: the leader, Noor, was reviewing the notes, so the reduced relative phrase identified the intended person. Leila also wrote, “The examples selected by the group were placed beside the questions.” This reduced passive clause identified examples that the group had selected. Jo accepted both, but asked Leila to avoid stacking so many clauses that the main action became hard to find.

The final paragraph described a change in the circle. At the first meeting, Noor spoke for most of the opening minutes. At the next meeting, she asked members to read silently before discussion because the room was smaller. The difference showed an adjustment to conditions, not a contradiction that needed a new label. Leila concluded that Noor’s observable practice was to make the purpose of a question explicit and revise the format when evidence about the setting changed. She added that one fictional account could illustrate a pattern of action without explaining a whole person.

The writers read their descriptions aloud beside the open door. They checked every antecedent, every comma, and every participle. A useful profile, Jo reminded them, should give a reader enough evidence to question an interpretation. It should also leave enough room for the person in the story to act differently in a new situation.

### Questions

1. What evidence showed that Noor adapted to the café, and what did Leila avoid concluding from that change?
2. Explain the roles of **who** in “Noor, who the members trusted” and **whose** in “a page whose margin contained three concrete questions”.
3. Why did Jo ask Leila to split her first long sentence, and how do the two reduced clauses later in the passage attach to their subjects?
4. What was the final workshop’s limitation about describing a person, and how did the writers check that their grammar supported the meaning?

**Visible hints before answering:** (1) list the café changes and the exact conclusion Leila rejected; (2) identify the noun after **whose** and the verb after **who**; (3) compare the two ideas in the first draft, then ask who is reviewing and who is selected; (4) find the final limitation and the four grammar checks named at the end.

**Transfer (80–120 words):** Write a fictional 100-word profile combining one non-defining **who** clause, one **whose + noun** clause, one active reduced clause, and one passive reduced clause. Include two condition-linked observations and a final limitation about what the account cannot prove. Revise so each clause attaches to the intended noun.

<details><summary>Hidden key, evidence, and acceptable alternatives</summary>

1. Noor removed two questions when the meeting moved to the narrow café, asked readers to compare one passage at a time, and recorded the reduced space and time. Leila avoided concluding that the group was less interested or that Noor had a fixed new type; she kept the explanation tied to the changed setting.
2. **Who** refers to Noor and is the object of **trusted**; the non-defining commas add information about her. **Whose** refers to **page** and comes before **margin**, expressing the association between the page and its margin. The page’s margin contains the questions; it does not mean that the page owns a person.
3. The first sentence joined Noor’s trusted status and the notes without making their relationship clear, and the two coordinated ideas obscured the main action. The comma plus **and** correctly joins two main clauses; the problem is the unclear connection, not a comma splice. Splitting it clarified the evidence and reason. In “the leader reviewing the final notes”, **reviewing** modifies **leader** (Noor), corresponding to “the leader who was reviewing the final notes”; it is an active reduced relative phrase. A fronted phrase such as “Reviewing the notes, Noor …” would instead be an adverbial participle clause. In “the examples selected by the group”, the examples receive the selecting, so the reduced clause is passive and attaches to examples.
4. One fictional account can illustrate a pattern of action without explaining a whole person or predicting future behaviour. The writers checked antecedents, commas, subject/object roles, voice, and participle attachment so that the grammar did not overstate the evidence or hide the main action.

</details>

## Cycle-wide language check

For each day, underline one defining clause, one non-defining clause, one **whose** clause, and one reduced clause. Identify the noun each clause modifies, then label active or passive voice and completed or ongoing meaning for reduced clauses. Check that a full relative clause has the intended subject/object relationship and that commas mark non-defining information. A correct answer may use a semantically equivalent sentence when the attachment and evidence remain accurate.

## Source and boundary note

The passages, questions, keys, hints, and transfers are original course writing. The people, teams, rooms, and observations are fictional; they make no medical, CEFR, diagnostic, MBTI, Big Five, forecast, or empirical personality claim. Grammar explanations are bounded to the retained Grammar 5 file linked above. This author check does not certify independent acceptance, course completion, test readiness, or learner attainment.


<a id="sec-ielts-phase-3-writing-cycle5-writing-md"></a>
## Source 031 — `IELTS/Phase-3/writing/cycle5-writing.md`

# Writing — Cycle 5: an integrated personality-description workshop

Chu kỳ này kết hợp defining/non-defining clauses, **whose/where/when/why**, object-relative omission, và reduced clauses trong ba văn bản hư cấu. Mỗi bài yêu cầu **200–250 từ**. Dành 50 phút: hiểu nhiệm vụ và lập dàn ý (8), viết nháp (15), kiểm tra mệnh đề (10), sửa ý và dấu câu (10), tự chấm (7). Không dùng một nhãn để chẩn đoán hoặc dự báo một người; mọi kết luận phải gắn với hành vi và điều kiện quan sát được.

## Quy tắc chấm 0/1/2

Với từng tiêu chí trong sáu tiêu chí của ngày: 2 điểm nếu đáp ứng đầy đủ và chính xác, 1 nếu đúng một phần hoặc còn lỗi nhỏ, 0 nếu thiếu hoặc sai nghĩa. Tổng 12 điểm chỉ giúp xác định phần cần sửa; không chứng nhận trình độ. Viết tiếng Anh trước khi mở mẫu, giữ bản nháp và bản sửa. Một phản hồi khác vẫn hợp lệ nếu đáp ứng yêu cầu và nêu rõ bằng chứng.

## D17 — Hồ sơ làm việc cho người hướng dẫn

### Nhiệm vụ

Viết một hồ sơ 200–250 từ về một người hướng dẫn thảo luận hư cấu. Dùng một defining **who**, một defining object relative có thể bỏ đại từ, một non-defining **who/which**, một **whose + noun**, và một **where** hoặc **when**. Nêu hai hành vi trong hai điều kiện, đề xuất một hỗ trợ thực tế, và kết thúc bằng giới hạn diễn giải. Hồ sơ phải giúp đồng nghiệp biết nên làm gì, không phải gắn nhãn cố định.

### Quy trình và tự kiểm

1. Lập bảng: hành vi, điều kiện, mệnh đề cần dùng, và hỗ trợ có thể thử.
2. Viết defining clause để nhận diện người/vật; viết non-defining clause có hai dấu phẩy cho thông tin thêm.
3. Sửa: kiểm tra đại từ tân ngữ có thể bỏ, chủ thể sau **who** phải giữ, và mọi **whose** có danh từ theo sau.

**Gợi ý tăng dần (hiển thị trước mẫu):** (1) bắt đầu bằng “The mentor who…” và “the draft I…”; (2) thêm “Tara, whose notebook…” và “the library where…”; (3) thay một nhãn bằng hành vi trong hai điều kiện.

**Chuyển giao và tự chấm:** Viết thêm một câu hỗ trợ mà hồ sơ chưa nêu. Chấm 0–2 cho nhiệm vụ/độ dài, bằng chứng, defining clauses, non-defining/**whose**/dấu phẩy, **where/when** và tổ chức/giới hạn (12 điểm). Ghi một câu trước và sau khi sửa.

<details><summary>Mẫu gốc, chú giải và rubric đầy đủ</summary>

### Mẫu gốc (229 từ)

Work profile: Tara, a fictional discussion mentor

Tara is a fictional mentor who prepares one question before each discussion. The draft I observed contained two possible explanations, and Tara asked the writer to mark the evidence that supported each one. Tara, whose notebook has a page for questions and another for examples, works in a library where quiet discussion is possible. During a crowded meeting, she moved the group to a smaller table and asked everyone to write one point before speaking. During a quiet meeting, she invited immediate discussion and recorded the questions that the group raised.

These observations suggest a practice rather than a permanent type. Tara changes the order of discussion when the setting changes, and she asks for evidence before accepting a broad description. A colleague who works with her should send the draft before a busy meeting and identify the decision that needs group agreement. The support gives Tara and the group a clear starting point, but it does not prove that she will use the same method in every room.

I would keep the heading “asks for evidence” beside the examples, rather than replacing it with a personality label. The next meeting should add one observation from a different task. That record can refine the description, especially when a learner needs a quick answer or when the group must revise a shared plan.

### Chú giải và sửa câu

- **who prepares** là defining chủ ngữ; **that the group raised** là defining object relative và có thể viết *the questions the group raised*.
- **whose notebook** có danh từ sau **whose**; **where quiet discussion is possible** bổ nghĩa cho **library**.
- Bản yếu “Tara is patient” được sửa thành hai hành vi, hai điều kiện và một hỗ trợ có thể thử.

### Rubric và đáp án hợp lệ (12 điểm)

- 0–2: đúng 200–250 từ, hồ sơ có mục đích, hỗ trợ và giới hạn.
- 0–2: hai hành vi gắn với hai điều kiện.
- 0–2: defining **who** và object relative có vai đúng.
- 0–2: non-defining/**whose** và dấu phẩy chính xác.
- 0–2: **where/when** gắn đúng danh từ; tổ chức rõ.
- 0–2: sửa câu và giới hạn không dự báo.

Chấp nhận *the questions which the group raised* hoặc *the questions the group raised*. Chấp nhận **when** cho thời gian thay **where** cho nơi chốn. Mọi hỗ trợ thực tế khác đều đạt nếu xuất phát từ bằng chứng.
</details>

## D18 — Bản ghi biên tập một mô tả nhóm

### Nhiệm vụ

Viết bản ghi biên tập 200–250 từ về một nhóm hư cấu đang sửa mô tả một thành viên. Dùng **whose** cho người và vật, một **why** clause để nêu lý do, một **when** hoặc **where**, một active reduced clause, và một passive reduced clause. Bao gồm một object relative có thể bỏ đại từ và một defining clause giúp chọn đúng tài liệu. Giải thích một thay đổi trước/sau và giới hạn của bản ghi.

### Quy trình và tự kiểm

1. Gạch chân câu cũ, bằng chứng thực tế, và câu thay thế; không bắt đầu bằng nhãn.
2. Xác định danh từ làm chủ thể của **-ing** và danh từ nhận hành động trong **-ed**.
3. Kiểm tra **why** trả lời lý do, **when/where** trả lời thời gian/nơi chốn, rồi sửa câu có **whose + noun**.

**Gợi ý tăng dần (hiển thị trước mẫu):** (1) viết “the file I edited” để thử bỏ object relative; (2) dùng “the editor checking…” và “the notes collected…”; (3) đặt “why” sau *the reason* hoặc dùng câu “This is why…”.

**Chuyển giao và tự chấm:** Viết thêm một câu hỏi mà nhóm nên hỏi trước khi chốt mô tả. Chấm 0–2 cho nhiệm vụ/độ dài, bằng chứng, **whose/why**, **when/where**, active/passive reduced clauses, và sửa/giới hạn (12 điểm). Đánh dấu D/ND hoặc reduced bên cạnh từng câu.

<details><summary>Mẫu gốc, chú giải và rubric đầy đủ</summary>

### Mẫu gốc (236 từ)

Editorial record: a description needs conditions

The old draft called Eli reliable because he completed a checklist. The editor whose copy I reviewed had saved the file I edited, but the notes did not show when the checklist was used or where the shared equipment was stored. The reason why the group needed a new description was simple: a label hid the conditions that shaped the result.

The revised note says that Eli checks the list before a busy shift. The editor checking the labels asked him to record one missing item, while the notes collected by the team were placed beside the equipment map, whose headings identified each storage area. These are reduced clauses with clear attachment: the editor performs the checking, and the notes receive the collecting. The group also kept a sentence about a quiet shift, when Eli helped a new volunteer find the storage box. A single example from one shift should not define every future response.

For the next month, the team will record the task, the available time, and the action taken after a problem. Eli can correct the record, and another volunteer can add an example that complicates the first impression. This process may produce fuller descriptions during the month, but it cannot prove that a checklist caused every improvement or that Eli has one permanent quality. The useful revision is a question about conditions, not a final category.

### Chú giải và sửa câu

- **whose copy I reviewed** expresses the editor–copy relationship; **whose headings identified…** associates the map with its headings. **The file I edited** omits object **that/which**.
- **why the group needed…** gives a reason; **when Eli helped…** modifies **shift** as a time relation.
- **editor checking** is active and **notes collected** is passive. The model names attachment, but does not claim a participle alone supplies full tense or completion.

### Rubric và đáp án hợp lệ (12 điểm)

- 0–2: đúng 200–250 từ, bản ghi có vấn đề, sửa đổi và giới hạn.
- 0–2: bằng chứng gồm điều kiện, nhiệm vụ và hành động.
- 0–2: **whose/why** đúng vai và danh từ sau **whose**.
- 0–2: **when/where** gắn đúng quan hệ thời gian/nơi chốn.
- 0–2: active/passive reduced clauses gắn đúng chủ thể/đối tượng.
- 0–2: before/after cụ thể và giới hạn không khẳng định nguyên nhân duy nhất.

Chấp nhận *the file which I edited* hoặc *the file I edited*. Chấp nhận **why** trong cấu trúc lý do khác nếu quan hệ rõ. Chấp nhận động từ khác khi người/vật thực hiện và nhận hành động được giải thích.
</details>

## D19 — Reflection after a feedback workshop

### Nhiệm vụ

Viết bài phản tỉnh 200–250 từ về một workshop hư cấu, trong đó người hướng dẫn dùng câu hỏi để giúp nhóm mô tả feedback. Dùng một defining **who**, một non-defining **whose**, một **where/when**, một **why**, một object relative có thể lược bỏ, và hai reduced clauses (một active, một passive). Bao gồm ít nhất hai quan sát và một giới hạn cuối bài. Không dùng mô tả để giải thích chắc chắn toàn bộ con người.

### Quy trình và tự kiểm

1. Lập dàn ý theo thứ tự: bối cảnh, quan sát, chỉnh sửa câu hỏi, bài học, giới hạn.
2. Gạch D/ND/reduced bên cạnh mệnh đề; gạch chủ thể của mỗi phân từ.
3. Sửa: non-defining phải có dấu phẩy; object relative mới được bỏ; kiểm tra **whose + noun**, **where/when/why**, và câu không tạo attachment mơ hồ.

**Gợi ý tăng dần (hiển thị trước mẫu):** (1) dùng “the participant who…” để nhận diện; (2) dùng “the question I revised” để bỏ object; (3) kết thúc bằng “This fictional account does not prove…”.

**Chuyển giao và tự chấm:** Viết một câu hỏi mới cho workshop và một câu giới hạn khác. Chấm 0–2 cho nhiệm vụ/độ dài, bằng chứng, defining/object omission, non-defining/**whose**, **where/when/why**, reduced clauses/attachment (12 điểm). Giữ bản nháp và gạch một thay đổi có lý do.

<details><summary>Mẫu gốc, chú giải và rubric đầy đủ</summary>

### Mẫu gốc (230 từ)

Reflection after a feedback workshop

The workshop took place in a library where the group could compare drafts quietly. The facilitator who opened the session asked each participant to describe one comment that had helped and one comment that remained unclear. I revised the question I wrote after a participant asked why a broad label was being used. The participant, whose notes contained two concrete examples, explained that the label described the effect of a task rather than a permanent quality.

The facilitator checking the new questions invited the group to test one example at a time. The comments collected during the second discussion were placed beside the draft, so the writers could see which sentence each comment addressed. We added a space for the date when each comment was made and the reason why a change was requested. These choices made the conversation more precise, although they did not make every interpretation correct.

The workshop produced a useful procedure: name the sentence, identify the condition, and ask what could be changed next. The group should repeat the procedure in another meeting, which may reveal a different need. It would be inaccurate to say that the workshop discovered a fixed personality trait or that the facilitator’s method will solve every feedback problem. This fictional account shows how careful questions can connect observations with a revision while leaving the conclusion open.

### Chú giải và sửa câu

- **who opened the session** is a defining subject relative; **the question I wrote** omits object **that/which**.
- **whose notes** is non-defining because the participant is already identified; commas surround the extra information.
- **checking the new questions** is active and **collected during…** is passive, both modifying the intended nouns. **where** modifies **library**; **when each comment was made** modifies **date**, and **why a change was requested** modifies **reason**. In “asked why a broad label was being used”, **why** introduces an embedded question rather than a reason relative.
- A weak ending said, “The workshop proved the facilitator understood everyone.” The revision limits the claim to a procedure and fictional account.

### Rubric và đáp án hợp lệ (12 điểm)

- 0–2: đúng 200–250 từ, đủ bối cảnh, phản tỉnh, và giới hạn.
- 0–2: ít nhất hai quan sát cụ thể.
- 0–2: defining **who** và object omission đúng.
- 0–2: non-defining/**whose** và dấu phẩy đúng.
- 0–2: **where/when/why** đúng nghĩa và reduced clauses gắn rõ.
- 0–2: before/after có lý do, tự sửa, và không khẳng định chắc chắn.

Chấp nhận mệnh đề tương đương nếu xác định đúng antecedent và vai chủ thể/tân ngữ. Chấp nhận đổi **in which/during which** cho **where/when** khi ý nghĩa giữ nguyên. Không chấp nhận **whose** đứng một mình hoặc bỏ đại từ chủ ngữ.
</details>

## Boundary note

Ba nhiệm vụ, mẫu, chú giải và rubric là nội dung gốc. Nhân vật, workshop và quan sát là hư cấu; không có khẳng định chẩn đoán, khoa học, CEFR, band score, thành tích người học hoặc độ sẵn sàng khóa học. Đây là author-side resource chờ independent review.


<a id="sec-ielts-phase-3-speaking-cycle5-speaking-md"></a>
## Source 032 — `IELTS/Phase-3/speaking/cycle5-speaking.md`

# Speaking — Cycle 5: integrated fictional personality observations

Đây là ba buổi D17–D19. Dùng các cấu trúc đã học trong [personality description workshop](#sec-ielts-phase-3-grammar-05-personality-description-workshop-md) và vocabulary core hiện hành trong [Cycle 5 vocabulary](#sec-ielts-phase-3-vocabulary-05-personality-cycle5-md). Không thêm core vocabulary mới. Mọi trait/state/context là quan sát hư cấu có giới hạn, không phải chẩn đoán hay kết luận khoa học.

## Quy trình đúng 50 phút cho mỗi buổi

1. **Phút 0–8:** đọc prompt, chọn 2–3 chi tiết có nghĩa và lập dàn ý ba gạch đầu dòng.
2. **Phút 8–18:** chuẩn bị đúng **1 phút**, nói **60–90 giây**, rồi nghe bản ghi của chính mình và ghi một chỗ khó hiểu.
3. **Phút 18–28:** chọn đúng **hai lỗi** có thể sửa; ghi câu trước và câu sau.
4. **Phút 28–40:** chuẩn bị lại đúng **1 phút**, nói lại **60–90 giây**, nghe lại và ghi một thay đổi có ích.
5. **Phút 40–50:** tự chấm 10 điểm, điền error log và chọn bước tiếp theo.

**Rubric 10 điểm dùng cho mọi buổi:** prompt và 2–3 chi tiết (0–3: 0 bỏ prompt; 1 có ý nhưng quá ít chi tiết; 2 đủ prompt và hai chi tiết; 3 chi tiết cụ thể nối với lý do); mệnh đề/antecedent và ý nghĩa (0–3: 0 không có hoặc sai nghĩa; 1 có nhưng antecedent/attachment mơ hồ; 2 hầu hết đúng; 3 đúng và giải thích được quan hệ); từ core đã học dùng đúng ngữ cảnh (0–2: 0 không có cách dùng phù hợp; 1 có từ phù hợp nhưng còn lỗi hoặc thiếu ngữ cảnh; 2 dùng chính xác và tự nhiên); dễ hiểu và tự sửa (0–2: 0 không hoàn tất; 1 hoàn tất nhưng chưa sửa rõ; 2 bản thu lại rõ hơn). **7–10:** sang buổi tiếp; **5–6:** sửa hai lỗi và thu lại một lần; **dưới 5:** dùng dàn ý ba câu, luyện câu mẫu, rồi nói lại. Accent không bị trừ điểm; chỉ ghi lỗi làm người nghe khó hiểu.

**Error log:** `Ngày/buổi:` · `Prompt:` · `Lỗi 1 và câu trước:` · `Sửa 1:` · `Lỗi 2 và câu trước:` · `Sửa 2:` · `Thay đổi nghe rõ hơn trong bản thu lại:` · `Điểm/10 và bước tiếp theo:`

Nhân vật và nơi chốn là hư cấu. Có thể thay bằng trải nghiệm cá nhân hoặc giữ hoàn toàn hư cấu; không cần nói thông tin riêng tư. Các bài mẫu là spoken English tự nhiên, không phải bài giảng ngữ pháp.

## D17 — A fictional preparation routine

**Prompt:** Describe a fictional discussion leader preparing a meeting. Include 2–3 meaningful details: an observable trait-like tendency, a current state, and a context that may affect the action. Use defining/non-defining clauses and one whose or where clause. Speak for 60–90 seconds (120–180 words).

<details><summary>Gợi ý 1 — chọn ý</summary>

nói một hành động cụ thể thay cho nhãn; thêm trạng thái hôm đó và nơi diễn ra

</details>
<details><summary>Gợi ý 2 — kiểm tra cấu trúc</summary>

“the leader who checks…” can identify one person; “Mai, who…” adds information; whose links a person to a noun and where links a place

</details>
<details><summary>Gợi ý 3 — giới hạn diễn giải</summary>

tách trait-like pattern khỏi state/context: một buổi chuẩn bị không giải thích mọi lựa chọn

</details>

<details><summary>Full model (delayed)</summary>

**Natural spoken model (143 words):**

“Mai is a fictional discussion leader who checks each question before a meeting. I met her in a library where the group could speak quietly, and her notebook, whose pages held short examples, stayed open beside the schedule. She looked tired that afternoon because she had travelled across town, yet she still asked the group to separate an observation from a label. One participant wanted a quick description, but Mai asked what the person actually did and when it happened. I would describe her as careful in this setting, not as someone who is always careful. The library, which had a busy entrance, also affected the conversation because people paused when the door opened. We could repeat the discussion in another place and compare the conditions. That later comparison might refine the fictional account, but it would not prove a permanent personality type.”

</details>

**Actual clause and meaning check:** “who checks each question” identifies the fictional leader; “where the group could speak quietly” modifies library; “whose pages held…” links notebook pages to the notebook. Tired is a state tied to that afternoon, while careful is a cautious description of observed action, not a diagnosis.

**Worked before/after correction:** Before: “Mai, whose checks each question, leads.” After: “Mai, who checks each question, leads.” **Whose** must be followed by a noun, such as “Mai, whose notebook holds examples, leads.” The correction keeps the intended antecedent and meaning.
## D18 — A fictional feedback exchange

**Prompt:** Tell a fictional story about a feedback exchange. Include 2–3 meaningful details: a person whose notebook or task shows a habit, a place or time that shaped the exchange, and a question you would ask next. Use defining and non-defining clauses plus where/when/why. Speak for 60–90 seconds (120–180 words).

<details><summary>Gợi ý 1 — chọn ý</summary>

chọn một notebook hoặc task làm bằng chứng; nói rõ where/when; kết thúc bằng câu hỏi tiếp theo

</details>
<details><summary>Gợi ý 2 — kiểm tra cấu trúc</summary>

whose + noun; where follows a real place noun; when follows a real time/event noun; do not force every clause into one sentence

</details>
<details><summary>Gợi ý 3 — giới hạn diễn giải</summary>

asked why = embedded question asks for a reason; a relative why means “for which” after a noun such as reason

</details>

<details><summary>Full model (delayed)</summary>

**Natural spoken model (155 words):**

“In a fictional feedback exchange, I spoke with Nhi, whose notebook listed two questions for every draft. We met in a small studio where the team displayed its sketches. Nhi chose the draft that showed a clear contrast, but she was quiet when the room became crowded. I asked why she had selected that example, and she said that the reason why it helped was its simple order. Nhi pointed to the first sketch and compared it with the final version. She invited the writer to record one question before changing the layout, so the team could check the choice later. I would ask whether the same choice would appear in a quieter session, because one crowded meeting cannot explain a broad tendency. Nhi’s notebook suggests a habit of asking for evidence, but it does not prove that she will respond in the same way every time. A later fictional meeting could add another observation.”

</details>

**Actual clause and meaning check:** “whose notebook listed…” has Nhi as antecedent; “where the team displayed…” modifies studio; “when the room became crowded” is a time clause. “why she had selected that example” is an embedded question after asked. In “the reason why it helped,” why is a relative adverb linked to reason. These meanings must not be swapped.

**Worked before/after correction:** Before: “I asked the reason why she selected it.” After: “I asked why she selected it.” The first can be grammatical but is less direct here; the second is the embedded question. For a relative clause, use “the reason why it helped” with reason as the antecedent.
## D19 — A fictional role change

**Prompt:** Describe a fictional team member whose role changed during a project. Include 2–3 meaningful details: an observed pattern, a temporary state or constraint, and a place or time that matters. Use a mix of defining/non-defining, whose, where/when, and a reduced clause if useful. Speak for 60–90 seconds (120–180 words).

<details><summary>Gợi ý 1 — chọn ý</summary>

đưa ra hành động và bối cảnh trước khi mô tả pattern; chọn một role change cụ thể

</details>
<details><summary>Gợi ý 2 — kiểm tra cấu trúc</summary>

kiểm tra antecedent của every who/which/whose/where/when; reduced clause phải có chủ thể rõ

</details>
<details><summary>Gợi ý 3 — giới hạn diễn giải</summary>

nói “in this project” hoặc “that afternoon”; không biến vai trò tạm thời thành nhãn cố định

</details>

<details><summary>Full model (delayed)</summary>

**Natural spoken model (146 words):**

“Quang is a fictional team member whose role changed when the project lost one afternoon of rehearsal. The person who usually checked the schedule took the role of presenter, and Quang, who had prepared the visual notes, moved to the timing table. We met in a room where the clock was easy to see, although the room was noisy near the door. Quang looked hesitant at first, but he asked a precise question when the group changed the order. The notes prepared by Quang helped the presenter find the next example, and the team finished the short practice. I would ask how he would work with a full rehearsal before describing any wider tendency. This observation shows a useful response in one context; it does not prove that Quang prefers or dislikes a permanent role. The team could record another session when the time limit changes.”

</details>

**Actual clause and meaning check:** “whose role changed” links role to Quang; “who usually checked…” identifies the person; “where the clock was easy to see” modifies room; “when the project lost…” gives the time/condition. “prepared by Quang” is passive reduced and modifies notes. Hesitant is a temporary state in the fictional session.

**Worked before/after correction:** Before: “Quang, where prepared the notes, changed roles.” After: “Quang, who had prepared the notes, changed roles.” Where requires a place antecedent; a person needs who. The corrected clause matches the actual antecedent and keeps the comma boundaries.


<a id="sec-ielts-phase-3-reading-cycle6-reading-md"></a>
## Source 033 — `IELTS/Phase-3/reading/cycle6-reading.md`

# Reading — Cycle 6: cumulative review through changing conditions

This review cycle revisits the relative-clause, possessive-clause, omitted-object, and reduced-clause skills from Cycles 1–5. The three passages are original fiction about people working together. They do not diagnose a person, forecast behaviour, or make claims about CEFR or personality science. Spend 50 minutes per day: preview and glossary (5), first reading (12), four questions (15), evidence check (10), and transfer writing (8). Score each question 0–2 (8 points), then add 0–2 for the transfer’s evidence and grammar, for 10 points per day. Record a first score, use the visible hints, and record a revised score.

## Cách học và tự chấm

Dành khoảng 50 phút mỗi ngày: xem tiêu đề và từ khó (5), đọc lần đầu (12), trả lời bốn câu (15), kiểm tra bằng chứng và gợi ý (10), viết đoạn chuyển giao (8). Làm bài trước khi mở đáp án. Xác định danh từ được bổ nghĩa và vai trò chủ ngữ hoặc tân ngữ; phân biệt mệnh đề quan hệ rút gọn với mệnh đề phân từ chỉ hoàn cảnh. Mỗi câu: 2 điểm nếu đúng ý và giải thích hoặc bằng chứng, 1 nếu đúng một phần, 0 nếu sai hoặc chưa trả lời. Đoạn chuyển giao: 1 điểm cho bằng chứng và giới hạn, 1 cho ngữ pháp và sự gắn kết đúng. Tổng 10 điểm mỗi ngày dùng để tìm phần cần sửa; không chứng nhận trình độ. Ghi điểm lần đầu và sau khi sửa.

## D21 — The rota that needed a reason

### Passage (fictional, 526 words)

The museum’s evening team used a rota that assigned one person to greet visitors and another to check the display cases. The rota worked until a school group arrived with a guide who needed access to a locked cabinet. Mara, who was greeting visitors, left the entrance after telling the next volunteer where she was going. The change helped the guide, but it left the entrance without a named replacement. A visitor waited beside the desk while two volunteers searched for the key.

At the next meeting, the coordinator showed the incident report that Mara had written. The report described the request, the movement of the key, and the time that the entrance had been unattended. It did not describe Mara as careless. Mara said that she had assumed another volunteer would watch the desk because the rota listed the team rather than the person responsible at each moment. Jo, whose job was to revise the rota, agreed that the missing information concerned ownership, not a fixed quality in Mara.

Jo proposed a new column called “next action”. The person who left a station would name the person receiving it, and the person receiving it would repeat the instruction. The rule sounded formal, but the team tested it during a busy afternoon. Mara handed the desk to Arun, who repeated that he would greet the next group. When a guide requested a cabinet key, Arun asked Suki to accompany the guide while he remained at the entrance. The handover took less than a minute.

The team also reviewed a sentence on the visitor card: “Please ask the volunteer needing help.” Jo changed it to “Please ask the volunteer who is available to help.” She explained that the original **-ing** clause was grammatical, but it could suggest a volunteer who was already needing help rather than a volunteer able to assist. The new full relative clause made the intended role clearer. The comma before **and** in the report remained in a sentence that joined two complete ideas; Jo said the conjunction made that coordination clear.

During the following week, the entrance was quiet on some evenings and crowded on others. The new handover was useful in both situations, although the reasons differed. In a quiet period it prevented uncertainty; during a crowd it prevented two people from assuming that the other had taken responsibility. Jo kept the incident report beside the rota because a rule was easier to understand when its reason was visible.

Mara read the final note before signing it. It said that she had responded to a real request with incomplete ownership information, and that the team had changed the system. The note did not erase the unattended entrance, and it did not predict how Mara would act in another museum. It gave the next team a concrete practice: name the receiver, repeat the handover, and record what remains uncertain.

The next rota included a space for the receiver’s initials and a space for a question that still needed checking. Those additions took seconds to complete, yet they allowed the team to revisit an assumption without blaming the person who first noticed the problem.

### Questions

1. What information was missing from the original rota, and how did the new “next action” column address it?
2. Why did Jo prefer “the volunteer who is available to help” to “the volunteer needing help” on the visitor card?
3. In “the incident report that Mara had written” and “the person who left a station”, identify the antecedent and subject/object role of each relative word.
4. What limitation did the final note preserve about interpreting Mara’s action?

**Visible hints before answering:** (1) find who was expected to watch the desk; (2) compare who is helping with who may be needing help; (3) ask who performed **written** and **left**; (4) reread the final two sentences.

**Transfer (80–120 words):** Describe a fictional team handover. Include one omitted object relative, one subject relative that remains, and one sentence explaining what an incident report cannot prove. Self-score evidence (0–1) and clause accuracy (0–1).

<details><summary>Hidden key and acceptable alternatives</summary>

1. The rota named the team but not the person responsible at each moment. The new column required the person leaving a station to name a receiver and the receiver to repeat the instruction. “It made ownership explicit” is acceptable.
2. “The volunteer needing help” can be read as the volunteer who needs help, while the card intended a volunteer able to help. The full clause makes the intended role explicit. The answer should not say that every **-ing** clause is wrong.
3. **That** refers to **report** and is the object of **had written**, so “the report Mara had written” is possible. **Who** refers to **person** and is the subject of **left**, so it cannot be omitted in the intended structure.
4. It preserved that Mara responded to a real request with incomplete ownership information; it did not erase the unattended entrance or predict how she would behave elsewhere. A response must include both accountability and the limit on generalisation.
</details>

## D22 — The notes beside the window

### Passage (fictional, 502 words)

The reading circle met in a library room whose windows faced a construction site. On quiet mornings, the members discussed a story’s structure around one table. On afternoons when drilling began, they moved to a smaller room where the shelves reduced the noise. The facilitator, An, asked the group to keep notes that separated what a reader noticed from what a reader inferred.

Ben brought a paragraph that described a character who changed plans twice. He called the character unreliable. An asked him to underline the actions that supported the word. Ben marked a cancelled appointment and a later message offering a different time. Linh, who had written the message, explained that the first appointment had overlapped with a family duty. The second change occurred after the other person requested a public place. The details did not remove the inconvenience, but they changed the question from “What type is this character?” to “What conditions shaped these choices?”

The group revised the paragraph. “The character, who cancelled the first meeting, sent a new message,” Ben wrote. An asked whether the commas were correct. They were: the clause added information about a named character rather than identifying which character. Ben then wrote, “The message that Linh received explained the new time.” Here **that** identified the message and acted as the object of **received**. The group compared the two sentences to see how punctuation affected meaning.

During the next meeting, Linh brought a chart whose headings had been copied from an earlier project. One heading said “calm readers”. Linh replaced it with “readers pausing before a reply”. An asked whether the reduced phrase described a stable trait. Linh said it described an observable action in the meeting. A member who preferred quick discussion could pause before replying; a member who usually wrote first could also do so. The phrase named an event, not an inner explanation.

The group then checked a sentence that began, “Reviewing the difficult paragraph, An asked for one concrete example.” An explained that this opening was an adverbial participle clause. It described the circumstance in which An asked the question; it was not a reduced relative clause modifying An. The subject understood from the opening was An, so the attachment was logical. Another sentence read, “The examples selected by the group were placed beside the chart.” That reduced passive clause modified **examples**, which received the selecting.

Before leaving, the members wrote a short rule for future meetings: record the action, the condition, and the interpretation separately. The construction noise had changed the room, but it had not changed every reader in the same way. The notes beside the window therefore contained both a useful description and a question about what the next meeting might show.

An added a final example to the margin. A reader who paused because the drill was loud might need a quieter room; a reader who paused because the sentence was complex might need a different explanation. The same visible pause could therefore invite different follow-up questions.

### Questions

1. How did the group replace a fixed label with a more evidence-based question about Ben’s character?
2. Why are the commas correct in “The character, who cancelled the first meeting, sent a new message”?
3. In “the message that Linh received”, what is the role of **that**, and how would omission change the sentence?
4. Distinguish the opening clause in “Reviewing the difficult paragraph, An asked…” from the reduced clause in “The examples selected by the group…”.

**Visible hints before answering:** (1) list the two appointment changes and their conditions; (2) ask whether the clause identifies which character; (3) identify who received the message; (4) ask what the opening clause describes and which noun receives the selecting.

**Transfer (80–120 words):** Write two sentences about a fictional meeting: one with a non-defining relative and one with a reduced passive clause. Add an adverbial participle opening whose subject is clear. State one interpretation that remains uncertain. Self-score evidence (0–1) and attachment accuracy (0–1).

<details><summary>Hidden key and acceptable alternatives</summary>

1. The group listed the cancelled appointment and later message, then added the family duty and request for a public place. It changed the question from a type label to the conditions shaping the choices. A response may say the label was replaced with an evidence-and-context question.
2. **The character** is already identified, so the clause adds extra information and is non-defining; commas are required. Removing it leaves “The character sent a new message.”
3. **That** refers to **message** and is the object of **received**; Linh received the message. It may be omitted: “The message Linh received explained the new time.” The answer must not treat **that** as the subject.
4. “Reviewing the difficult paragraph” is an adverbial participle clause describing the circumstance of An’s asking; it does not modify An as a reduced relative. “Selected by the group” is a reduced passive relative clause modifying **examples**, which receive the selecting. The forms alone do not prove a past tense; context supplies timing.
</details>

## D23 — The description that stayed open

### Passage (fictional, 510 words)

The final review asked the writers to produce a short profile of a fictional mentor who worked with a mixed-age study group. The profile had to connect actions with conditions and end with a limitation. Nhi chose Dara, whose notebook contained questions from six meetings. She began with a sentence that read, “Dara is patient, organised, and good with people.” The sentence sounded positive, but it did not show what Dara had done.

Nhi returned to the notes that Dara had made after a meeting held in a crowded café. Several learners had spoken at once, so Dara asked each person to write one point before the group discussed it. The next meeting took place in a quiet room where the group could hear a recording. Dara allowed spoken discussion to begin immediately and asked learners to compare the recording with their notes. The two meetings showed adjustments that were connected to conditions.

Nhi drafted a fuller description: “Dara, who changes the order of discussion when the room changes, asks for evidence before accepting a broad claim.” The non-defining clause added information about Dara, while the main clause gave the profile’s central practice. Nhi then added, “The questions written after the café meeting helped the group separate observation from opinion.” The reduced passive clause identified questions that someone had written; it did not say when the writing happened by grammar alone.

The draft included a possessive detail: “Dara kept a folder whose first page listed the points that the group still needed to check.” Nhi checked the antecedent of **whose**. It was **folder**, and the noun after **whose** was **first page**. The phrase did not mean that Dara owned every point or that the page had a personality. It linked a folder with its first page. The defining clause **that the group still needed to check** identified which points were unfinished.

During peer review, Quang suggested replacing “Dara understands every learner” with “Dara asks what support would make the next step clearer.” Nhi accepted the revision because the notes showed questions and adjustments, not complete knowledge of other people. A second reviewer suggested that the profile should call Dara “introverted” because she often wrote before speaking. Nhi kept the observation but declined the label. Writing first could be a useful preparation choice in those meetings; it did not explain every future setting.

The final paragraph said that Dara’s method helped the group notice evidence, choose a next step, and revisit an earlier interpretation. It also said that one fictional profile could illustrate a practice without proving a permanent trait. Nhi read the paragraph aloud and checked that each relative clause had an antecedent, each reduced clause had a logical attachment, and each comma matched the intended meaning. The description stayed open because good evidence can guide a question without closing it too soon.

They kept the first drafts in a folder marked “revisions”, because the route from observation to description was part of the lesson. A changed sentence showed which evidence had become clearer and which conclusion still required patience.

### Questions

1. What evidence replaced Nhi’s first list of positive adjectives?
2. What does the non-defining clause in “Dara, who changes the order…” add to the profile?
3. What is the antecedent of **whose** in the folder sentence, and what does the following defining clause identify?
4. Why did Nhi keep the observation about writing first but decline the label “introverted”?

**Visible hints before answering:** (1) compare the café and quiet-room observations; (2) remove the comma clause and identify the main claim that remains; (3) find the noun before **whose**, then ask which points the **that** clause identifies; (4) distinguish a preparation choice observed in these meetings from a prediction about every setting.

**Transfer (80–120 words):** Write a fictional profile that combines one non-defining **who** clause, one **whose + noun** clause, one active reduced clause, and one passive reduced clause. Include two observations under different conditions and a final limitation. Self-score evidence (0–1) and attachment accuracy (0–1).

**Additional D23 review:** Revisit the café and quiet-room notes, test the main sentence after removing its comma clause, identify the folder/first-page relationship and the unfinished points, and explain why an observed preparation choice does not establish behaviour in every setting.

<details><summary>Hidden key and acceptable alternatives</summary>

1. She used the café procedure of writing one point before discussion, the quiet-room procedure of comparing a recording with notes, and the related change in discussion order. These actions and conditions replaced unsupported adjectives.
2. It adds the observed practice that Dara changes discussion order when the room changes. Because Dara is already named, the clause is non-defining and the commas signal additional information; the main clause still says she asks for evidence.
3. **Whose** refers to **folder**, and **first page** is the noun associated with it. “That the group still needed to check” is a defining clause identifying which points were unfinished. It does not modify Dara or imply that the page has a personality.
4. Writing first was observed in the meetings and could be useful preparation, but the evidence did not explain Dara’s behaviour in every future setting. Nhi kept the condition-linked observation and avoided turning it into a permanent type claim.
</details>

## Cycle-wide self-check

For each day, mark one full relative clause, one omitted-object opportunity, one non-defining clause, one **whose** clause, and one reduced clause. For every clause, name its antecedent or subject and explain the evidence it adds. Remember that a comma plus **and** can coordinate complete ideas when the conjunction is present; an opening participle can be adverbial rather than a reduced relative; **-ing** does not by itself guarantee an ongoing event; and a past participle does not by itself encode completion. Score 0–2 for each day’s transfer and record one sentence you revised.

## Source and boundary note

The passages, questions, keys, hints, and transfers are original fictional course writing. They make no medical, diagnostic, CEFR, empirical personality, or forecasting claim. This review cycle adds no grammar or core vocabulary. It is an author check only and does not certify independent acceptance, test readiness, course completion, or learner attainment.


<a id="sec-ielts-phase-3-writing-cycle6-writing-md"></a>
## Source 034 — `IELTS/Phase-3/writing/cycle6-writing.md`

# Writing — Cycle 6: cumulative consolidation

Chu kỳ này ôn tập defining/non-defining clauses, **whose/where/when/why**, object-relative omission, và reduced clauses qua ba nhiệm vụ hư cấu. Mỗi mẫu tiếng Anh dài 200–250 từ. Dành 50 phút: đọc đề và lập dàn ý (8), viết nháp (15), kiểm tra bằng chứng và mệnh đề (10), sửa (10), tự chấm (7). Nhân vật và quan sát chỉ là hư cấu; không dùng chúng để chẩn đoán, dự báo, hoặc khẳng định CEFR, band score hay khoa học tính cách.

## D21 — Email bàn giao quyết định

### Nhiệm vụ

Viết email 200–250 từ cho một nhóm dự án hư cấu sau khi một quyết định bị hiểu khác nhau. Nêu hai quan sát trong hai thời điểm hoặc địa điểm, đề xuất một quy trình bàn giao, và kết thúc bằng giới hạn. Bắt buộc có: một defining subject relative, một defining object relative có thể bỏ đại từ, một non-defining **whose**, một **where** có antecedent nơi chốn, và một reduced clause gắn rõ danh từ.

### Quy trình và tự kiểm

1. Lập ý theo thứ tự: sự việc, bằng chứng, quy trình, giới hạn.
2. Đánh dấu S/O/D/ND/reduced bên cạnh từng mệnh đề; hỏi ai thực hiện động từ.
3. Sửa câu: kiểm tra **whose + noun**, dấu phẩy non-defining, và object relative trước khi bỏ.

**Gợi ý tăng dần:** (1) dùng “the colleague who…” cho chủ thể; (2) dùng “the note I…” cho tân ngữ; (3) dùng “the folder, whose…” và một danh từ nơi chốn với **where**.

**Chuyển giao và tự chấm:** Viết một câu thay thế cho quy trình. Chấm 0–2 cho nhiệm vụ/độ dài, bằng chứng, defining/object omission, non-defining/**whose**, **where**/reduced attachment, và tổ chức/giới hạn (12 điểm). Giữ câu trước và sau khi sửa.

<details><summary>Original model, worked revision, and full rubric</summary>

### Model (214 words)

Subject: Clarifying the next handover

The colleague who opened the archive at nine recorded the decision that the team made after the morning review. I checked the note she left, but the note I received did not identify who would update the shared folder. The folder, whose first page listed three unfinished questions, was stored in the office where we kept the project maps. At lunchtime, a second volunteer found a different copy and assumed that the earlier decision had been cancelled.

I recommend a short handover sentence at the end of every meeting. The person who records the decision should name the next editor, and the next editor should repeat the task before leaving. The file selected for revision can then be placed beside the decision note. This routine will make ownership visible when the room is busy or when a new person joins the project.

The incident gives us useful evidence about the record, not a permanent description of either volunteer. The colleague who opened the archive acted promptly, while the volunteer who found the second copy acted on information that was incomplete. We should test the handover in another meeting and record what remains unclear. One incident cannot prove that either person will respond in the same way in every project.

### Annotation and before/after revision

- **who opened the archive** is a defining subject relative. **that the team made** and **the note I received** are object relatives; the latter omits **that/which**.
- **the folder, whose first page…** is non-defining, with antecedent **folder**. **where we kept the project maps** has antecedent **office**.
- **selected for revision** is passive reduced relative modifying **file**.
- Before: “The volunteers were disorganised.” After: the email names the missing editor, incomplete information, and a testable handover.

### Rubric and acceptable alternatives (12 points)

For each criterion, 0 = absent or misleading, 1 = partly accurate, 2 = complete and supported. Six criteria × 2 = 12: task/length; evidence; defining/object omission; non-defining/**whose**; **where** and reduced attachment; organisation/limitation.

Accept a different project, place, or handover if antecedents and roles are clear. Accept **the note that I received** or **the note I received**. Do not accept **whose** without a noun or a reduced clause with an unclear attachment.
</details>

## D22 — A reflective memo on a changed workspace

### Nhiệm vụ

Viết memo phản tỉnh 200–250 từ về một nhóm hư cấu chuyển phòng học. Giải thích hai quan sát gắn với thời gian/nơi chốn, đề xuất một điều chỉnh, và nêu một giới hạn. Bắt buộc có **when** với antecedent thời gian, **why** với quan hệ lý do, một non-defining **which**, một active reduced clause, và một passive reduced clause. Bao gồm một object relative có thể bỏ đại từ.

### Quy trình và tự kiểm

1. Lập hai cột cho phòng cũ/phòng mới, rồi ghi thời gian và tác động quan sát được.
2. Khoanh antecedent của **when/which**; gạch chủ thể của mỗi phân từ.
3. Sửa: **why** phải trả lời lý do; reduced clauses phải bổ nghĩa danh từ; không biến participle thành thì tự động.

**Gợi ý tăng dần:** (1) viết “the afternoon when…”; (2) dùng “the plan we changed” cho object omission; (3) ghép “the editor checking…” với “the notes collected…”.

**Chuyển giao và tự chấm:** Viết một câu chuyển **when** thành *during which*. Chấm 0–2 cho nhiệm vụ/độ dài, evidence, **when/why/which**, object omission, active/passive attachment, và revision/limitation (12 điểm). Đánh dấu antecedent trong bản sửa.

<details><summary>Original model, worked revision, and full rubric</summary>

### Model (213 words)

Reflective memo: the workshop room

The workshop began in the room where the group usually compared drafts. On the afternoon when construction started beside the building, several learners could not hear the recording. We moved to a smaller room, which had shelves that reduced the noise, and changed the plan we prepared in the morning. The reason why the group needed a second plan was practical: listening required quiet, while discussion required enough space for notes.

The editor checking the questions asked participants to write one example before speaking. The examples collected during the first discussion were placed beside the draft, so writers could see which sentence each comment addressed. The new arrangement helped some readers, but the narrow tables made it harder to spread out two documents. At the next meeting, we should reserve the quiet room for listening and use a larger table for comparison afterwards.

This memo describes two settings and the conditions recorded in them. It does not prove that a room determines how anyone participates, or that the new arrangement will solve every difficulty. We should ask what each person could hear, note when a task became difficult, and revise the plan after another session. A clear description keeps the evidence visible while leaving the next decision open.

### Annotation and before/after revision

- **when construction started beside the building** modifies the time noun **afternoon**. **why the group needed a second plan** gives the reason.
- **which had shelves…** is non-defining and refers to **smaller room**. **the plan we prepared** omits an object relative pronoun.
